Extracting memorized pieces of (copyrighted) books from open-weight language models
A. Feder Cooper
Stanford University &Aaron Gokaslan Institute for Foundation Models, MBZUAI &Ahmed Ahmed Stanford University Amy B. Cyphert West Virginia University College of Law &Christopher De Sa Cornell University Mark A. Lemley∗
Stanford Law School &Daniel E. Ho Stanford University &Percy Liang Stanford University
Corresponding authors: afedercooper@gmail.com, mlemley@law.stanford.edu
Abstract
Plaintiffs and defendants in copyright lawsuits over generative AI often make sweeping, opposing claims about the extent to which large language models (LLMs) have memorized plaintiffs’ protected expression in their training data. Drawing on both machine learning and copyright law, we show that these polarized positions dramatically oversimplify the relationship between memorization and copyright. To do so, we extend a recent probabilistic extraction technique to measure memorization of 
50
 books in 
17
 open-weight LLMs. Through thousands of experiments, we show that the extent of memorization varies both by model and by book. With respect to our specific extraction methodology, we find that most LLMs do not memorize most books—either in whole or in part. However, we also find that Llama 3.1 70B entirely memorizes some books, like the first Harry Potter book and 1984. In fact, the first Harry Potter is so memorized that, using a seed prompt consisting of just the first few tokens of the first chapter, we can deterministically generate the entire book near-verbatim. We discuss why our results have significant implications for copyright cases, though not ones that unambiguously favor either side.

1Introduction
In the dozens of copyright lawsuits over training large language models (LLMs), both sides tend to present opposing, simplified interpretations of the underlying technology. Plaintiffs—such as book authors—say that LLMs are giant copy machines that store (infringing) copies of their specific works and recombine them in outputs [kadrey]. Defendants—typically tech companies—claim that LLMs merely contain non-expressive “statistical correlations” [concordanthropic], and therefore do not copy any particular creative work. We show that the situation is more complicated than either side suggests.

During training, an LLM processes pieces of text in its training data and learns probabilistic relationships across the dataset as a whole. This informal intuition aligns with the “statistical correlations” that defendants describe as encoded in an LLM’s weights. However, sometimes, for some training data, these learned patterns are not so high-level or abstract; rather, they are highly specific [cooper2024files]. For reasons that are not fully understood, certain training data are not so transformed by training: they are “memorized” by the model—encoded in some form inside the LLM’s weights [carlini2023quantifying, lee2023talkin, cooper2024files, carlini2025blog]. Such memorized content can sometimes be “extracted” later; they can be reproduced in an LLM’s outputs at generation time [carlini2021extracting, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, nasr2023scalable].

Models do not memorize all of their training data, but they do memorize some of it. An LLM may therefore memorize parts of some plaintiffs’ copyrighted works, but not others’. When such memorization occurs, it involves copying that may have significant consequences for ongoing copyright litigation. Extraction generates a “copy” of training data, but it also demonstrates the existence of a “copy” of that training data is memorized inside the model itself. The model being a “copy” (in a technical sense that copyright cares about) has important implications. Notably, a model—not just extracted training data—could be deemed an infringing “copy” of the training data it has memorized [cooper2024files, lee2023talkin]. Copyright law offers the destruction of infringing materials as a remedy. So, just as courts have ordered the destruction of bootleg DVDs, they could order the destruction of infringing models [lee2023talkin, pamscience, townsend2024deletion].

When seeking such remedies, plaintiffs often cite technical papers that demonstrate memorization by extracting training data from open-weight models and production systems. But this reasoning is flawed for a key reason: existing research does not quantify the kinds of information most relevant to a copyright infringement claim [cooper2024files]. This work typically shows that on average models memorize some portion of their training data; it does not show how much models memorize specific works.

To address this gap, we investigate the extent to which open-weight models memorize verbatim content from copyrighted books. For our experiments, we use the Books3 dataset, a (now notorious) torrented corpus of nearly 
200
,
000
 books [gao2020pile, reisnerbooks3, lee2023talkin]. This dataset is known to be included in the training data of several LLMs [biderman2023pythia, touvron2023llamaopenefficientfoundation, llama2, llama3], and has been a focus of copyright litigation [reisnerbooks3, kadreyamendedconsolidated]. To gauge whether a particular sequence of text is memorized, we leverage a recent probabilistic extraction method [hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels] that computes the risk of extracting that sequence at generation time (Section 2).

Extraction risk matters for copyright because the practicability of producing memorized data in a model’s outputs may bear on infringement claims (Section 3). Building on this connection, we develop and validate a procedure that uses probabilistic extraction to surface as much memorization as possible within a book; it uses a sliding window to reveal how much and where memorization occurs, and quantifies the risk that a model will reproduce verbatim sequences in its outputs (Sections 4 & 5). We then run thousands of experiments on 
50
 books and 
17
 LLMs (Section 6). Our results show that, while most models do not memorize most books either in whole or in part, some models memorize some books entirely, such as the first Harry Potter book [hp1] and 1984 [1984]. Following from these results, we show how to deterministically reconstruct a near-perfect copy of the first Harry Potter using Llama 3.1 70B (Section 7). Last, we discuss the significant implications of these findings for copyright cases, though not ones that unambiguously favor either side. Our results complicate defendants’ fair use story, but they also complicate plaintiffs’ efforts to bring class action lawsuits (Section 8). In summary:

• Section 2. We provide relevant background on memorization and extraction risk [hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels].
• Section 3. We connect memorization and extraction risk to “copies” in U.S. copyright law.
• Section 4. We present our sliding-window procedure, which computes extraction risk throughout a book, and enables specific claims about how much an LLM has memorized specific books.
• Section​ 5.​​ We validate that our approach captures true instances of extraction (and thus memorization), not spurious cases where a model generates verbatim non-training data by chance.
• Section 6. We then run experiments for 
17
 LLMs on a set of 
50
 books in Books3, which surface novel insights about how book-specific memorization varies across models and books.
• Section 7. We reconstruct a near-perfect copy of the first Harry Potter using Llama 3.1 70B.
• Section 8. We offer key takeaways for copyright law and policy.
2Quantifying memorization through extraction
Extraction and memorization are related concepts, but differ in subtle ways. While extraction refers to reconstructing specific training data in a model’s generated outputs, memorization is broader: it involves reconstructing specific training data by examining the model through any means [cooper2023report, cooper2024files].

Successful extraction of a piece of training data in a model’s generation is evidence of memorization of training data inside the model [cooper2024files, carlini2025blog, schwarzschild2024rethinking, lee2023talkin, feldman2020mem]. This is uncontroversial in machine learning (ML). As Carlini explains, when a sufficiently large and unique piece of training data is extracted, “the only possible explanation is that the model has somewhere internally stored [that piece of training data]” [carlini2025blog]. As Cooper and Grimmelmann note, “in order to be able to extract memorized content from a model at generation time, that memorized content must be encoded in the model’s parameters. There is nowhere else it could be. A model is not a magical portal that pulls fresh information from some parallel universe into our own” [cooper2024files, p. 25].

Models do not memorize all of their training data, but they do memorize some of it. And so, an LLM may memorize parts of some plaintiffs’ copyrighted works, but not others’ (Sections 4–6). To show this, we first need more background on the probabilistic extraction metric we use in most of our experiments (Section 2.1) and provide guidance on its interpretation (Section 2.2).

2.1Probabilistic discoverable extraction
Refer to caption
Figure 1:Generating the exact completion of a quote from The Great Gatsby [gatsby] with Llama 1 30B.
Refer to caption
Figure 2:Plotting

Extraction of training data in a model’s outputs—rather than direct inspection of a model’s parameters—is the most common form of evidence used to quantify memorization in LLMs [nasr2025scalable, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, prashanth2024recite, carlini2023extracting, lee2022dedup, zhang2023counterfactual]. The standard metric in many frontier model release reports [reid2024gemini, gemma2, llama3] measures verbatim discoverable extraction: given a sequence of text from the training data, split it into a prefix and target suffix, prompt the model with the prefix, and deem extraction to be successful if the LLM reproduces the suffix exactly [carlini2021extracting, carlini2023quantifying, nasr2023scalable]. On its own, this type of method is well-suited to completion models that continue the prompt text (as in Figure 1), rather than instruction-tuned chatbots that reply in a conversational style. We therefore only study completion models, referring to these models by name, version, and size. For an example, the middle row of Figure 1 shows verbatim extraction of a quote from The Great Gatsby [gatsby] using Llama 1 30B (Appendix A.1).

Discoverable extraction reduces memorization to a binary (yes-or-no) outcome by issuing a single prompt to the model and checking whether the deterministic, greedy-decoded generation matches the target suffix. But LLMs are probabilistic: under realistic, non-deterministic decoding schemes (e.g., top-
k
 sampling), a model can produce different outputs in response to the same prompt, just as in Figure 1. As cooper2024files note, a copyright-relevant claim is therefore more likely to be concerned with how often a target suffix can be extracted, i.e.:

a model, when (a) given a particular type of input, will (b) produce a particular type of memorized output, (c) with a particular probability. That probability could be …1% … [or] … 35% …. The issue for copyright law …is what to do …with the fact that element (c)—the probabilistic element—is inescapable [cooper2024files].

We elaborate on copyright-specific aspects of this point in Sections 3 and 8. For now, we note that hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels introduce a metric called probabilistic discoverable extraction (probabilistic extraction, for short), which captures this notion. 3Memorization, extraction risk, and U.S. copyright
The dozens of pending copyright lawsuits concerning LLMs [chatgptiseating] generally present three interrelated issues: (1) whether training an LLM on copyrighted material is permitted fair use (i.e., limited use of the copyrighted material can, under certain circumstances, be used without permission from the copyright owner); (2) whether the model itself is a copy or derivative work (technical terms in copyright law) of the works on which it is trained (Section 8); and (3) whether the model reproduces copyrighted material in its outputs. Some suits present only one issue, while others present all three questions. Some suits are based on content owned by a single company, while others are class action lawsuits purporting to represent all book authors.4 These lawsuits have primarily focused on (1) training and on (3) model outputs, and the two decisions so far have held that training an LLM can be fair use, albeit with significant limitations [kadreyjudgment, bartzjudgment]. They also raise issues about the acquisition, storage, or use of shadow libraries of copyrighted material as training data, such as Books3 [reisnerbooks3, lee2023talkin].

Our work does not speak to the first issue; training and fair use have been discussed extensively elsewhere [lemley2021fairlearning]. Rather, our findings affect the other two issues: we show that copies are sometimes made in the model itself (i.e., memorized) and those copies can sometimes be generated as outputs (i.e., extracted). Because many LLMs are known to have been trained on Books3, we use books from this dataset to study memorization and extraction of copyrighted material (Sections 4–6).

Memorization and copies inside the model.  If an LLM memorizes all or a substantial portion of a copyrighted work (near-)verbatim, the LLM itself may be or contain an infringing copy or derivative work [lee2023talkin, cooper2024files]. This is because memorized training data are encoded inside the model (Sections 1 & 2). Others have argued that encoding the work in the form of model weights satisfies the technical definition of “copy” in the U.S. Copyright Act [cooper2024files]. In a technical sense, that copy may be of interest to copyright even if there is no practical way to access or extract it. Like the copies of copyrighted works used as training data during training, that copy in model might be fair use, but the analysis of fair use would look somewhat different than internal use of copyrighted works in training processes. This is particularly true for open-weight models like DeepSeek and Llama models, which are not merely used internally by their developers, but are themselves shared externally with others (Section 8).

Extracting copies in model outputs.  Because some memorized works in the training data are extractable, LLMs can sometimes output copies of those works to users [cooper2023report, lee2023talkin, henderson2023fair, lee2024talkinshort, cyphert2024memlaw, sag2024fairness, charlesworth2025]. Works with higher extraction probabilities exhibit greater extraction risk; it takes fewer attempts to produce an output that is a verbatim copy of such a work (Section 2.2). That output at generation time will be judged separately from the model itself, and is less likely to be a fair use [henderson2023fair, lee2023talkin].

Extraction is inherently probabilistic under realistic decoding schemes (Section 2), so the relevant question is not only whether a copy could ever appear for some prompt, but also how often that copy appears in order to be “meaningful” [cooper2024files]. When we measure suffix probabilities 
p
𝒛
 that are very close to 
0
%
, we do not count those sequences as extracted; one could perhaps understand such generations as instances of “a monkey at the typewriter” [borelmonkey, simpsons] (Section 5). Very large 
p
𝒛
 (e.g., 
>
90
%
) indicates near-certain reproduction; this is clearly extraction (and therefore evidence of memorization). However, most cases are not this clear, and it is not immediately obvious where to draw a line in the enormous range between these extremes. To avoid false positives, we use a conservative threshold 
τ
min
=
0.1
%
 for scientific evidence of extraction (Sections 2.2 & 5). But for copyright law and policy, the practicability of extraction may require a different bar: if reproducing a work just once would require thousands of prompts, the real-world risk may be relatively limited, even if nonzero (Section 8).

Altogether, setting sensible copyright policy requires a specific understanding of both of these sets of issues. However, ML research does not tend to investigate these types of work-specific questions. In the next section, we begin to address this gap by quantifying memorization of individual books.

4Measuring memorization of individual books
Memorization is the main focus in the next three sections. While the methods we use rely on extraction of 
50
-token suffixes (Section 2), we center our claims on what these measurements reveal about memorization inside model weights. We briefly address LLM outputs later (Section 7). To estimate how much a given model memorizes a given book, we introduce a sliding-window procedure that computes probabilistic extraction along the length of a book (Section 4.1). This procedure surfaces book-specific, contextual information about memorization—information that is not readily apparent from average extraction rates over randomly sampled sequences, which prior work typically relies on to quantify memorization (Section 4.2). In the subsequent sections, we assess the validity of our approach (Section 5) and demonstrate how it enables comparisons across models and books (Section 6).

To identify regions of memorization, we develop the following “panning for gold” approach. For a given book, we start at the beginning of the text file in Books3. We take a chunk of text that is sufficiently long to contain 
100
 tokens, slide 
10
 characters forward in the book text and repeat this process. We do this for the entire length of the book, which results in approximately 
1
 sequence every 
10
 characters, e.g., The Great Gatsby has 
270
,
870
 characters and thus roughly 
27
,
000
 sequences. For most of our experiments, we follow the standard procedure of evaluating 
100
-token sequences [carlini2023quantifying, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels], which we divide into 
50
-token prefix prompts and 
50
-token suffixes that we attempt to extract (Equation 1).

As a result, every character in the book (except for at the beginning and end) is present in multiple sequences—in both prefixes and suffixes. This significant degree of overlap is deliberate. Since we typically do not know how open-weight models were trained, it is not exactly clear how we should break up books into token sequences when attempting extraction. By testing various sequence starts and ends in the same area, we aim to surface as much total memorization as possible. We are attempting to surface extraction “hot-spots” within a book (Appendix D.2).

In Figure 3, we provide visualizations of the results of this procedure for one setting: Llama 3.1 70B (trained on Books3) on 1984 [1984] (a book contained in Books3) using top-
k

As alternate view, Figure 3(b) condenses the same results into a heatmap. Unlike the scatterplot, this heatmap does not convey how many sequences are extractable at different probabilities in each area of the book. Instead, at each character position, we plot the maximum extraction probability 
p
𝒛

As we show in Section 6, such heatmaps facilitate comparisons of memorization “hot-spots” across models at the same book location. This heatmap shows high extraction probabilities exist throughout the entirety of 1984. On its own, this does not mean that the whole book can be extracted in one continuous segment at generation time; these results show how much of the book we estimate to be memorized in the model’s parameters, with respect to probabilistic extraction (Equation 1) measured with 
50
-token prefixes and suffixes. We return to reconstructing an entire book at generation time in Section 7.

Our sliding-window procedure provides a detailed, work-specific view of memorization that is distinct from prior work in ML. That is, while prior work shows that models memorize certain amounts of the data they were trained on [e.g., carlini2021extracting, nasr2023scalable, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, lee2022dedup, biderman2023pythia, reid2024gemini, gemma2, biderman2023emergent], it typically quantifies memorization through overall extraction rates—much like those in Figure 4 (left). For some (part of a) training dataset, researchers draw (typically at random) a set 
ℤ
 of sequences 
𝒛
 of a specified length (e.g., 
100
 tokens). For each sequence, they prompt with its prefix and count extraction as successful if the resulting generation matches the target suffix (Section 2). The extraction rate is computed as the number of attempted extractions that succeeded, relative to the total number of attempts:

These reported averages are generally small, just as we observe in Figure 4 (left) for 
9
 models trained on Books3. The highest rate we observe on random 
100
-token sequences (
50
-token prefix 
+
 
50
-token suffix) from Books3 is just over 
0.6
%
 (for Llama 3.1 70B). Small average extraction rates often form the basis of defendants in copyright infringement suits calling memorization a rare “bug” [cooper2024files, openairesponse]. However, while average extraction rates are useful for estimating overall memorization of Books3 text, they clearly tell an incomplete story. Low extraction rates signal that models likely do not memorize most text in Books3, but specific works can be highly memorized. On average, Llama 3.1 70B may have only memorized about 
0.6
%
 of sequences in Books3, but concealed within that small number is that it memorized effectively all of 1984 (Figure 3).

We can see this intuitively in Figure 3; target suffixes whose extraction probability exceeds 
τ
min
=
0.1
%
 can be found nearly everywhere in the book.5 However, we can also make this more concrete: using the results of our sliding-window procedure, we can quantify how much of a whole book is extractable. We compute extraction coverage as the fraction of characters in a book that lie within at least one suffix whose extraction probability

5Validating our extraction procedure
In prior sections (Section 2–4), we emphasized that the minimum probability we count as successful extraction, 
τ
min
 (Equation 2), is deliberately conservative. But how can we be confident this is the case: that when we measure a successful extraction, we are capturing a true instance of memorization, rather than happenstance generation of a verbatim suffix (a “monkey at the typewriter” event, Section 3)? We address this question through a set of experiments designed to validate our approach. As before, we apply our sliding-window procedure with top-
k
 decoding (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
) and 
50
-token suffixes (Section 4.1), but here we also vary prefix length (up to 
800
 tokens) to observe how conditioning on additional context affects our measurements.

Refer to caption
Figure 6:Evaluating Llama 3.1 70B, Llama 3.1 8B, and Phi 4 on We Were Eight Years in Power [wewere] (a book in Books3), using prefix lengths in 
{
25
,
50
,
100
,
200
,
400
,
800
}
 and 
50
-token suffixes with top-
k
 decoding (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
). The book is in Llama’s training data, but not Phi 4’s. Longer prefixes reveal more memorized sequences, though not all sequences in the book are extractable. For Phi 4, the only extractable suffixes are widely duplicated quotes that likely appeared in its training data from other sources. These results support that when our procedure identifies extraction, it is detecting true instances of memorization rather than chance generations.
As a baseline, we run experiments on a book from Books3 and Llama 3.1 models, which were trained on Books3 (Section 5.1). As expected [carlini2023quantifying], longer prefixes surface more memorized training data. Then, we run a set of negative controls: we use the same procedure for models on non-training data, which tests whether our method spuriously registers false positives on data where memorization is impossible (Section 5.2). Unlike the experiments on training data, regardless of prefix length, suffix probabilities remain below 
τ
min
 (with interesting exceptions9), demonstrating our approach does not mistakenly register non-training data as extractable. Together, these experiments show that, when our measurements indicate extraction, we can be confident they are a valid reflection of memorization. We briefly discuss the implications of these results (Section 5.3), which inform the experiments we run in the following section. Additional results and discussion can be found in Appendix E.

5.1Longer prefixes surface more memorized training data
When a sequence is known to be in the training data, memorization is the most plausible explanation for high-probability generation of its (sufficiently long) verbatim suffix given the corresponding prefix (Section 2). So, as a baseline, we apply the sliding-window procedure to this setting (Figure 6): we attempt to extract sequences in We Were Eight Years in Power [wewere] (a book in Books3) from Llama 3.1 70B and Llama 3.1 8B (models trained on Books3). Longer prefixes reveal additional memorized sequences. This is what carlini2023quantifying call the discoverability phenomenon: providing additional context (a longer ground-truth prefix) can raise the conditional probability of the target suffix 
p
𝒛
 enough that it becomes extractable (
p
𝒛
≥
τ
min
). However, many sequences remain unextractable: no matter how long the prefix, their suffix probabilities 
p
𝒛
 never surpass 
τ
min
.10

For example, Llama 3.1 70B memorizes most of the book, yet consistent sections remain unextractable across all prefix lengths (e.g., white gaps in Figure 6, such as from 
∼
75
K–
120
K characters). The same is even clearer for Llama 3.1 8B: being smaller, it memorizes significantly less [carlini2023quantifying, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, carlini2021extracting]. Longer prefixes do surface more memorized training data, but most of the book remains unextractable. These results are consistent with prior work: high-quality models do not—indeed cannot [lee2022dedup, carlini2021extracting, mahdavi2024memorizationcapacitymultiheadattention, collins2017capacitytrainabilityrecurrentneural]—memorize all of the training data in their massive training datasets. Even as prefix length increases, our measurements do not suddenly capture extraction success for everything; they capture success for sequences that have been memorized.11 We further support this point in the next section, where we compare these results to experiments on non-training data.

5.2Measurements on non-training data do not mistakenly register extraction
Using the above results as a basis for comparison, we next test our method in settings where memorization is impossible. This is the role of negative controls: experiments in conditions where the outcome of interest should not occur. If (false) positives appear in such a setting, they indicate that the measurement procedure is capturing spurious signal. In our context, running the sliding-window procedure on non-training data provides this sanity check. In general (with important exceptions noted below), observing suffix probabilities above 
τ
min
 would indicate false positives, i.e., would count successful extractions where none should exist. The absence of such cases is evidence that the extractions we do count are valid reflections of memorization.

We run two sets of negative controls. First, we apply our procedure to Books3 books with Phi 4 [phi4]—a model that was not trained on whole copyrighted books. Second, we run Llama 3.1 70B (trained on Books3), on books from 2025, i.e., books that post-date its 2024 training cutoff. In our plots, we distinguish these conditions by color: blue for models trained on Books3 on Books3 text, orange for Phi 4 on Books3 text, and gray for results on post-cutoff books.

Negative control 1: A model not trained on Books3 
×
 Books3 text.  We run the same experiment as in Section 5.1 on Phi 4, which was not trained on in-copyright books like We Were Eight Years in Power [wewere]. To support the validity of our measurement approach, unlike with Llama 3.1 models, we should not observe extraction success for unique text from this book. Our results for Phi 4 in Figure 6 confirm this expectation: increasing the context length does not push the conditional probability of non-training-data suffixes high enough to cross the 
τ
min
 threshold. We do observe increased extraction signal, but it only concentrates in four small areas that do not appear to be false positives. Instead, they plausibly reflect memorized text: the suffixes our procedure counts as extracted are famous quotes that are widely duplicated on the Internet, and thus very likely to have been included in Phi 4’s training data via sources other than this copyrighted book [phi4]. For example, at 
∼
280
K characters, we extract sequences containing passages from the King James Bible [deuteronomy]; at 
∼
546
K characters, we extract a quote from Barack Obama’s 2004 Democratic National Convention speech [obama2004]. Notably, Llama 3.1 models also exhibit high extraction probabilities in these same regions. While they were trained on this book, it is plausible that duplicates from other sources contributed to the memorization and extractability of these sequences [lee2022dedup].

) has 
p

≥

 (the faint gray band at 
∼

13
 orders of magnitude smaller than our setting for 
τ
min
. With a single attempt, the odds of generating the verbatim suffix are 
1
/
p
𝒛
≈
1
/
10
−
14
%
, or 
1
 in 
10
 quadrillion (Appendix E.4.2). To give a sense of just how small this number is, the chance of winning the Powerball jackpot in a single draw is about 
1
 in 
292
 million—roughly 
34
 million times more likely.

5.3Implications for measuring probabilistic extraction on books
Three important observations follow from these results (and related experiments in Appendix E.4); we highlight them here and defer additional discussion to Appendix E. First, the negative controls provide strong evidence that our sliding-window probabilistic extraction procedure is valid. Second, they indicate that our choice of 
τ
min
 for counting extraction success is indeed very conservative. Given the very low probabilities we observe for generating non-training data (Figure 7, Appendix E.4.2), we could reasonably set 
τ
min
 below 
0.1
%
. As such, our results almost certainly under-count memorization, and should be read as a lower bound. Nevertheless, we prefer to err on the side of caution. To lower 
τ
min
 and remain confident about validity, we would need to run a much larger set of negative controls, which exceeds the compute budget for this work. Part of the reason is that, as others have noted [cooper2024files], LLMs are not actually like monkeys randomly outputting tokens. They have learned to generate structured, grammatical sentences, so the distribution of plausible outputs is far smaller than random chance would suggest (Appendix E.5). Exploring what this implies for how high 
p
𝒛
 must be to count as evidence of memorization is an important question for future work. For now, we prefer to under-count memorization rather than risk over-counting it.

Third, having validated our procedure, we can now apply it more broadly. In particular, we are justified in extending our experiments beyond models like Llama, where we know that Books3 was part of the training data. If suffix probabilities for Books3 sequences surpass 
τ
min
, it is reasonable to interpret these as true extractions and thus evidence of memorization. This remains the case regardless of whether the model was literally trained on Books3, or whether the memorized training data originate from other overlapping sources—for example, highly duplicated famous quotations, as we saw with Phi 4 in Figure 6 (Appendix E.6). On this basis, in the following section, we expand our analysis to include 
3
 additional models for which it is unknown if Books3 was included in the training data.

6Comparing memorization across books and models
We now present the main results of applying the sliding-window method. We describe the experimental setup (Section 6.1) and highlight the types of comparisons our approach facilitates across both books and models (Section 6.2). We defer discussion of the copyright implications of these results to Section 8.

6.1Experimental setup
We use the same setup as in Section 4.1: the sliding-window procedure with top-
k
 decoding (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
) for 
100
-token sequences (
50
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes). We run this procedure on 
17
 models, which we organize into three groups: (a) models trained on Books3 (Llama [touvron2023llamaopenefficientfoundation, llama2, llama3] and Pythia [biderman2023pythia], blue); (b) models that were very likely trained on (at least parts of) books also contained in Books3, based on measurements of 
p
𝒛
≥
τ
min
=
0.1
%
 for unique content from those books (DeepSeek v1, Qwen 2.5, and Gemma 2, red); and (c) a model not trained on copyrighted books (Phi 4, orange). Of the 
∼
200
,
000
 books in Books3, we include 
50
 in our analysis. These are the same books for which we analyze extraction coverage in Figure 5. They include works associated with plaintiffs in the amended class action complaint of Kadrey et al. v. Meta, Inc. [kadreyamendedconsolidated, pp. 4-5],12 as well as generally popular books [e.g., hp1, hobbit, sisyphus, catch22]), and some academic texts [e.g., zittrain, dante]). We suspected that some popular books would exhibit higher degrees of memorization (e.g., due to duplicated text from other sources). This is why we did not select books at random; we deliberately chose titles intended to capture variation across Books3. Further details on these decisions are in Appendix D.1. We ran thousands of experiments on these 
50
 books and 
17
 models (Appendix D.3). (In total, these experiments cost 
6
 GPU months.) Nevertheless, our results should not be read as a complete account of memorization across the entire Books3 dataset.

6.2Key patterns in our results
We focus here on only a few representative books and on the first two categories of models—(a) models known to be trained on Books3 and (b) models where it is not disclosed whether Books3 was included in the training data. Recall that extraction probability 
p
𝒛
 reflects the expected frequency that a model would reproduce the verbatim 
50
-token suffix given its prefix (Section 2). Because of this, 
p
𝒛
 values can be compared across models, allowing us to evaluate which models are more or less at risk of outputting verbatim 
50
-token suffixes.13 With this metric, we draw conclusions about different degrees of memorization: how memorization varies for a specific book across models and across books for a given model. We omit heatmaps for Llama 3 70B, as they resemble those for Llama 3.1 70B.

Refer to caption
(a)Models trained on Books3
Refer to caption
(b)Models that we conclude were trained on (at least parts of) some books that are also contained in Books3
Figure 8:Heatmaps clarify how memorization varies widely across books and models. We apply the sliding-window procedure with top-
k
 decoding (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
) and 
100
-token sequences (
50
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes). We show (a) models known to be trained on Books3 (blue) and (b) models for which it is unknown if Books3 was included in the training data (red). With respect to these settings, Llama 3.1 70B memorizes effectively all of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone.
Comparing a single book across models.  Each book in Figure 8 illustrates a different high-level pattern of memorization across models. There are books like M. Butterfly [mbutterfly], for which our sliding-window procedure reveals almost no memorization: aside from the copyright notice, we extract little—if any—text from these books across models of different sizes trained by different organizations. Most books we test with 
50
-token prefixes resemble this pattern. However, for a given book, we are often able to extract some substantive sequences from Llama 3 and 3.1 70B.

Books like Beloved [beloved] represent a more intermediate case: for these books, memorization is generally low for most models with respect to 
50
-token prefixes (Appendix E.3), but with important exceptions. Llama 3 and 3.1 70B memorize a considerable number of fragments in the book. Other Llama models memorize significantly less, and category (b) models (DeepSeek v1 67B, Qwen 2.5 72B, and Gemma 2 27B) memorize even less. For Beloved, the 
7
 largest models we test—category (b) and all large Llama models—memorize the same passages at 
∼
170
K [belovedquote1, belovedquote3] and 
∼
480
K characters [belovedquote2]. Both are famous quotes from the book, which we confirmed are highly duplicated on the Internet. As with Phi 4 (Section 5.2), models that otherwise exhibit low memorization (except for such passages) may not have been trained on the whole book; rather, they may have memorized these passages due to their inclusion and duplication in other sources (Appendix E.6).

Last, select books exhibit enormous degrees of memorization for the 
7
 largest models, with lesser amounts of memorization in smaller models like Llama 1 13B. These are widely popular books like Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone [hp1] (Figure 8) and 1984 [1984] (Figure 3). While memorization of such books tends to be significant for larger models, it is especially high for Llama 3 and 3.1 70B, which appear to memorize several books in their entirety [e.g., hp4, gatsby, ulysses, hobbit].

Comparing different books within a model.  Small models generally memorize less than larger ones, but there is variation across books. For example, Llama 1 13B memorizes more of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone than M. Butterfly and Beloved; Llama 3.1 8B memorizes portions of We Were Eight Years in Power [wewere] (Figure 6), but less from other books (Appendix E.3). Variation is more pronounced in larger models. As noted above, for all models, most results resemble those shown in Figure 8 for M. Butterfly. This is true for most of the plaintiff-authored books in Kadrey et al. v. Meta, Inc. [kadreyamendedconsolidated]—with exceptions (e.g., Llama 3.1 70B on wewere; see Figure 6). Extraction coverage distributions reinforce this conclusion: they skew toward low coverage (Section 4.2, Appendix D.4). However, there are clearly several exceptions. Llama 3 and Llama 3.1 70B memorize significantly more from the 
50
 books than any other model we tested. Many of these books yield results similar to those for Beloved [e.g., handmaid, davinci, sisyphus, curious]. In extreme cases, both models appear to memorize entire books, such as Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone. We address this further in Section 7. Llama 1 65B, Llama 2 70B, and category (b) models also exhibit high memorization for some, but not all, of these books.

Altogether, these patterns underscore the importance of training choices in shaping memorization. Models do not memorize all of their training data [lee2022dedup, carlini2021extracting, mahdavi2024memorizationcapacitymultiheadattention, collins2017capacitytrainabilityrecurrentneural]. The mere existence of particular sequences within a training dataset does not imply a particular conclusion about memorization [lee2022dedup, lee2023explainers, lee2023talkin, lee2024talkinshort]. For example, similarly sized models trained by different organizations display different degrees of memorization of the same book—even when the evidence strongly suggests the entire book (e.g., Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone) was included in their respective training datasets (Appendix E.6). This is also evident with respect to just Llama models, which were all trained by the same organization (Meta) on Books3. All Llama models (by size) memorize more than the other models we evaluate. But notably, within size classes, newer versions typically memorize more from specific books than earlier ones: Llama 3 models appear to have memorized more from books than Llama 2, which in turn memorize more than Llama 1.

7Reconstructing Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone with one seed prompt
Our sliding-window probabilistic extraction experiments estimate how much of a given book is memorized within an LLM’s weights, with respect to 
100
-token sequences (
50
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes). For a 
50
-token suffix where 
p
𝒛
=
1
%
, it would take (in expectation) 
100
 prompts with the prefix to extract the verbatim suffix (Section 2.2). Therefore, these results do not imply that one would definitively be able to extract large, continuous portions of a book all at once during generation.

However, for Llama 3 70B and 3.1 70B on some books (Figures 3 & 8), our results show that there exist extraordinarily high extraction probabilities throughout. In fact, these probabilities approach 
100
%
 when we increase the prefix length. For example, for Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone, increasing the prefix to 
200
 tokens pushes 
p
𝒛
 (for 
50
-token suffixes) to over 
90
%
 almost everywhere in the book (Appendix E.3). This means we can extract almost any 
50
-token segment with approximately 
1
 prompt, using the corresponding 
200
-token prefix. With this degree of memorization across the entire book, one could possibly reconstruct the entire book near-verbatim, starting from a single short prompt of ground-truth text to seed the generation process. Using beam search to decode generations—which deterministically approximates the highest probability sequence under the model for a given configuration—the model could reasonably continue the seed prompt to complete the book.

7.1A simple reconstruction procedure
As a simple test of this idea, we implement this reconstruction procedure with about 
100
 lines of boilerplate generation code using HuggingFace APIs for Llama 3.1 70B. We begin with an 
s
-token seed prompt (e.g., 
s
=
60
, the first line of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone), and set a maximum sliding context length of 
n
 tokens. The model generates 
m
 tokens at a time using beam search, until reaching the context window length 
n
. At this point, we remove the first 
m
 tokens from the context and append the generated 
m
 tokens, creating a new 
n
-token prompt. This updated prompt becomes the input for the next generation step. We repeat this process, sliding along the length of the book by prompting with 
n
 tokens to produce the next 
m
 tokens. After the first 
n
 tokens, the clipped, 
n
-length context window progresses beyond the initial seed prompt. All subsequent prompts do not contain any ground-truth text drawn from the book; they consist entirely of generated text from prior iterations. In essence, this reconstruction procedure is just standard autoregressive generation. The only difference is that we clip the context to a fixed window length 
n
—rather than letting it grow by 
m
 tokens every iteration—by discarding the oldest tokens as new ones are generated.

With appropriate choices of 
s
, 
n
, 
m
, and configuration of beam search,14 we attempt to generate the entire book with high fidelity. On our hardware, after testing just a handful of configurations for beam search and a few different short seed prompts, we were able to deterministically generate a near-exact copy of the entire 304-page book. We show results in this section using a seed prompt of just "Mr. and Mrs. D" (
s
=
6
 tokens, drawn from the first line of the first chapter, prepended with the beginning-of-sequence token) and a beam width of 
k
=
8
; we use a sliding context length of 
n
=
3000
 tokens and generate 
m
=
50
 tokens at each step. In practice, the only minor complication was that Llama 3.1 70B tended to predict end-of-sequence (EOS) tokens at the ends of chapters. To address this, we removed the EOS token from the generated 
m
 tokens before appending them to the context, and replaced it with the tokens for "CHAPTER {c+1}" (with {c+1} spelled out in words, e.g., when 
c
=
2
, we insert "CHAPTER THREE"). With this simple adjustment, the model successfully continued to generate the book.

In summary, it was straightforward to produce a copy of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone that is nearly identical to the original. Further, because beam search is a deterministic decoding algorithm, our results are reproducible. We provide the complete (very short) code, and discuss the stability of this result and future work beyond this “existence proof” in Appendix F.

Refer to caption
Refer to caption
Refer to caption
Refer to caption
Figure 9: Samples of the diff between the ground-truth text of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone [hp1] from Books3 and the entire book text we generated using Llama 3.1 70B, starting with a short seed prompt from the first chapter. See Appendix F.1.
7.2Assessing the fidelity of the reconstructed book
Qualitatively, the two documents are close to identical. As illustrated in Figure 9, differences predominantly involve small inconsistencies in formatting: white space, capitalization, use of underscores (_) to indicate italics, etc. The version of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone in Books3 uses British spelling (e.g., "Mum" instead of "Mom"), so there are also some minor spelling differences. Very occasionally, when there is only a single line in a paragraph, the model skips that line during generation.

We also quantitatively assess the similarity of the two texts, using metrics for which two identical documents have a score of 
1
. The cosine similarity of the two documents’ TF-IDF vectors is a near-perfect 
0.9999
. TF-IDF is a limited metric, as it treats documents as bags of words and thus fails to capture word order. So, as two additional points of comparison, we compute similarity using greedy longest common subsequence matching at both the word and sentence levels (via difflib’s SequenceMatcher). We obtain a word-level similarity of 
0.992
 and a sentence-level similarity (which is more sensitive to formatting differences) of 
0.934
.15

8Takeaways for copyright law and policy
Our experiments show that the extent of memorization in models varies by model, model family, model size, the book tested, and even within different parts of an individual book (Section 4–6). These results complicate the narratives both plaintiffs and defendants typically present in copyright cases when describing how LLMs work. They support the positions of plaintiffs in some respects and of defendants in others. We see three primary implications for copyright disputes: LLMs could be derivative works of the books they memorize (Section 8.1); in some cases, it may be practical to generate infringing copies (Section 8.2); and, class actions are ill-suited for copyright claims about memorization (Section 8.3).

8.1LLMs could be derivative works of the books they memorize
In the case of some models, there is quite a lot of memorization of some books, though most books are not memorized at all—with respect to our specific extraction methodology—either in whole or in part. Such evidence of memorization in the model matters for the dispute over whether models themselves are derivative works (Section 3), an argument that courts have thus far not been receptive to [bartzjudgment, kadreyjudgment].

A work is not a derivative work unless it is “substantially similar” in significant part to the original work [e.g., litchfield]. While prior debates over whether models can qualify as derivative works have been largely theoretical [cooper2024files, lee2023talkin], the results of our probabilistic extraction procedure offer empirical evidence that is directly relevant to this issue. They indicate that models are not, as plaintiffs sometimes contend, mere copies of all of the works on which they were trained. However, they also suggest that at least some LLMs (e.g., Llama 3.1 70B) may be derivative works of at least some books (e.g., Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone)—because those LLMs have memorized a significant amount of protectable expression from those books. The law does not require that the entire work be included in the derivative; it is enough that the derivative incorporates a substantial amount of protectable expression. We find that this turns out to be true for some (but not all) books for some (but not all) models.

Fair use.  The inquiry does not end here; the model, like the training dataset, may be protected under copyright’s fair use doctrine [lemley2021fairlearning]. But the fair use analysis of the model itself may be different than the fair use analysis of the training dataset [lee2023talkin]. Copyright law treats intermediate copies, such as those used internally within a system, differently from external-facing copies. A sequence of copyrighted data used for training is an intermediate copy used internally by AI companies for model training, rather than sold as a product. Unless that sequence is extracted, it is only used internally in the course of the model producing outputs. Beyond extraction, individual sequences may to lesser or greater extents influence model outputs, but many of these outputs will not be substantially similar to any training data [henderson2023fair, lee2023talkin].

A model, too, is an intermediate work in many cases. If the model memorizes content but only generates noninfringing outputs, that is a strong argument that how the model makes use of that content is transformative and ought to be permissible fair use. But for companies that sell or release their models to others under open source licenses, the model itself is the product, and sometimes one that is being sold for commercial gain. That does not defeat the fair use argument, but it makes the analogy to training data less precise. In these instances, it may be harder to rely on the cases that justify training on copyrighted data as fair use [e.g., authorsguild, sega, sonyconnectix].

Potential consequences of a model being a copy of works it has memorized.  A finding that the model itself is a copy of some copyrighted works, and that a distribution of the model was thus a distribution of a copy of those works, could be dramatic for the AI industry. Llama 3.1 70B was downloaded 
105
,
029
 times from HuggingFace in May 2025 (Appendix G)—far less from the height of its release in 2024. If we say conservatively that the model has been downloaded 1 million times since its release,16 then those 1 million downloads could be seen as 1 million potentially infringing distributions of reproductions of Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone—as well as any other books in copyright for which a more than de minimis amount of copyrighted expression has been memorized.

In general, damages for copyright infringement can run very high: statutory damages can be as high as $150,000 per infringed work, and actual damages can be much higher. If the model itself is a copy and is not protected by fair use, courts might also order that it not be distributed or even that copies of the model itself be destroyed [17usc503]. We have only studied a relatively small number of books (
50
) and models (
17
), so cannot draw strong conclusions about the extent of memorization of books in general. In principle, if the overall quantity of memorized books in open-weight models is particularly high, it is not an exaggeration to say that the risk of damages across all of those books and of orders of destruction are an existential threat to the companies training these models.

Policy considerations.  The effect of these copyright rules may be to treat openly distributed models less favorably than models gated behind closed system, because the distributed models contain a latent copy or derivative of some copyrighted works. However, there is no good policy reason to treat open-weight models more harshly in copyright law than proprietary ones. Policymakers may want to act to protect models from being declared unlawful copies, in order to prevent crushing financial liability on developers for providing a model that generates transformative, noninfringing outputs. However, a blanket protection for all open-weight models may also be unwarranted. The extent to which models memorize their training data is significantly influenced by choices during training, such as de-duplicating the training dataset [cooper2024files, carlini2023quantifying, lee2023talkin, lee2022dedup, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels] Policymakers may be less inclined to protect models if the developers made training choices known to substantially increase the risk of memorization—for example, deliberately duplicating particular books.

8.2It may be practical to generate infringing copies in some cases
In our experiments with the sliding-window probabilistic extraction procedure (Sections 4 & 6), we use relatively short 
50
-token prompts drawn from books to see if it is possible for an LLM to generate verbatim the following 
50
 tokens with high probability. As is standard in ML, we use extraction of text snippets for evidence of memorization inside the model. But no one would realistically use this technique to extract an entire book. Doing so would require access to at least half of the book to use as 
50
-token prefixes in order to even attempt to produce the other half in 
50
-token suffixes. And, for sequences that are memorized, it might take in expectation up to 
1
/
τ
min
=
1
,
000
 tries to produce each verbatim suffix, with no indication of which of the many responses represents the actual text of the book.

Traditional extraction measurements vs. reconstruction of (whole) books.  Reliable extraction of large segments of text would require other approaches, like the reconstruction procedure that we apply to Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone with Llama 3.1 70B (Section 7). This result is an extreme case and is not representative of most of the other books we have tested (Appendix F.3). Nevertheless, it is significant: in contrast to claims by some defendants [concordanthropic], it illustrates that, for LLMs that exhibit high degrees of memorization of specific works, it may be possible to generate copies or derivative works of those works. General assessments of the extent of this type of behavior, and how easy it is to elicit it, require more detailed exploration.

Practicality of extraction and responsibility for infringement.  Even though our results suggest it may be possible to reconstruct large segments of copyrighted text from an LLM, they do not imply that it will always be easy to do so. This may affect who courts view as directly liable for any output infringement [lee2023talkin]. As a policy matter, the case for liability against the model developer is stronger if simple prompts generate infringing outputs. The case is weaker if a user (e.g., a plaintiffs’ lawyer) employs more intensive strategies to try to generate infringing material. In that case, the adversarial user—who perhaps runs hundreds or thousands of prompts to try to get one output that is infringing—seems more like a direct volitional actor misusing the model [costar]; they should likely bear some responsibility for the infringement.17 By contrast, in several cases (e.g., the first Harry Potter book, 1984), it does not take a thousand or even hundreds of generations to get large amounts of text; it takes only a handful. And in one case that we have identified so far (Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone), it is possible to generate the book directly from a short starting prompt and boilerplate generation code (Section 7). We will address this further in future work.

Considerations for LLM training and infringement.  The fact that extraction is probabilistic may affect resolution of the “is training infringement” issue. However, it is not determinative. Using a work as training data does not mean that it will necessarily be memorized. And, even if it is memorized, it may still be difficult to extract that work. Large parts of a work would need to be extractable in order to create superseding copies (i.e., a copy that can stand in for/ replace the work). Based on our current results, this seems unlikely (though possible, see Section 7) in practice for most works.

Nonetheless, the fact that memorization occurs at all creates a point of distinction from Authors Guild v. Google [authorsguild]. In this case, the court held that Google’s scanning of books to create a searchable database, and Google’s public display of limited text snippets on Google Books, was fair use. The court also considered and rejected an argument that hackers could gain unauthorized access to Google’s internal books database that supported the service, and thereby obtain plaintiffs’ entire works [804fd, at 227-28]. In contrast, our results show that it is possible to recreate some of the content of some books by “hacking” the model itself. We think it unlikely as a practical matter that people will use the model in this way; there are easier and more effective ways to pirate a book. But at a minimum, it complicates the copyright fair use analysis.

8.3Class actions are ill-suited for copyright claims about memorization
To be certified as a class action, plaintiffs generally have to show that all putative class members share common legal and factual issues, as well as suffer common injuries. Because many of the pending cases are proceeding as class actions, plaintiffs will therefore need to demonstrate that all book owners meet this standard so that a court can certify the class and treat their claims together [class].

As is clear from our results, this may be impossible. Basic questions about whether a particular model actually incorporated any significant expression from any individual plaintiff’s book cannot be generalized. Rather, it varies case by case: the extent of memorization differs widely from model to model, and even from work to work within a model (Section 6). This makes it difficult to assess, on a class-wide basis, whether a particular model copied a particular work and whether, for that model, infringing outputs based on memorization are even possible. Indeed, we show that memorization of works varies for many of the actual named authors in lawsuits, and even across books for the same plaintiffs (e.g., Ta-Nehisi Coates, see Figure 6; Appendices D.3.6–D.3.8, & E.3). Answering questions about the degree to which a specific LLM has memorized a specific work requires experimental evidence—for example, running every book through tests similar to the ones we performed here. Some plaintiffs may be able to show copying, but others will not. Courts generally deny class certification in such circumstances [e.g., walmart].

9Conclusion
Building on recent work on probabilistic discoverable extraction [hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels] (Section 2), we develop (Section 4) and validate (Section 5) a sliding-window procedure to estimate the extent to which open-weight models memorize different books contained in the Books3 training dataset. Our results show that the extent of verbatim memorization of books in open-weight LLMs is more significant than previously described. They also show that memorization varies widely from model to model and from book to book within each model, as well as varying in different parts of individual books (Section 6). We find that, with respect to our sliding-window probabilistic extraction procedure, most models do not memorize most books. But this is not always the case. In extreme instances, a model may memorize a book so significantly that it is possible to generate the entire book near-verbatim, as we have done with Llama 3.1 70B for Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone (Section 7).

Altogether, our results complicate current disputes over copyright infringement, both by rejecting easy claims made by both sides about how models work and by demonstrating that there is no single answer to the question of how much a model memorizes (Sections 3 & 8). There is much more work to do. We only ran book-specific, verbatim-extraction experiments for 
50
 books in Books3. In future work, we intend to test significantly more books and models, as well as possible explanations for memorization variability and for the high level of memorization of some specific works by particular models.

We provide additional notes on the background material in this paper, including the “careless people” quote (Appendix A.1), more details on the key probabilistic extraction metric in this paper (called

A.1Additional notes on the “careless people” example
The headline figure we include in Figure 1 (Section 2) is meant to be illustrative—an instance of, not a general statement about, memorization and extraction. This example also plays a role in some of our validity experiments (Section 5 and Appendix E). The text is drawn from The Great Gatsby [gatsby]; it is one of the most famous quotes from the book. It is also a short, 
57
-token sequence: the prefix is 
25
 tokens and the suffix is 
32
 (with the Llama 1 tokenizer). We do not focus on such short suffixes in our analysis, for reasons that are discussed both in the main text and in our experimental details below. While extraction of verbatim 
32
-token suffixes is often accepted as valid evidence for memorization [biderman2023pythia], it is now more common practice to use 
50
-token suffixes. (Regarding validity, also refer to Section 5 and Appendix E.) We compute the extraction probability with respect to top-
k
 sampling (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
). We do this by transforming the model logits according to the sampling parameters of this scheme. (See more about this below in Appendix A.3.)

It is also the case that very famous quotes like this are duplicated in many locations in training-data sources (e.g., blogs, essays, personal web pages, etc.) We are not claiming (here, or in general) that this sequence can be extracted because a given book (here, The Great Gatsby) was included in Books3; it could be extracted (or more extractable) because it is duplicated in many places in the training data. Of course, one of those copies is in Books3, if the entirety of Books3 is included in the model’s training data. We discuss this further in Appendix E.

, one can pick a probability threshold 
p
 and get the corresponding number of prompts 
n
 (or vice versa). Figure 10 (right) visualizes how 
n
 changes for different settings of 
p
 for the “careless people” quote.

In this paper, we re-frame 
(
n
,
p
)
-discoverable extraction to emphasize the quantity 
p
𝒛
 (A.2) that we actually compute: the probability of extracting a sequence 
𝒛
 (for the given LLM, hyperparameter-configured decoding scheme, and suffix start index location within sequence 
𝒛
). This is also useful for plotting distributions over 
p
𝒛
 for a given book—to see how probabilities vary for sequences across a book, or for a given sequence across different models. We provide such comparisons in Section 6.

In practice, for LLMs, we can compute 
p
𝒛
 for verbatim extraction without prompting 
n
 times. We compute sequence probabilities directly from the logits we obtain from running sequences through the LLM, rather than generating multiple suffixes for each prefix. (See Appendix A.3.) These two approaches are functionally equivalent for verbatim extraction: the computed sequence probabilities correspond to the statistically expected frequencies of verbatim extracted outputs we would observe if we were to generate a large number of sequences for the same prompt. hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, the authors of this extraction approach, confirm this with ample experimental evidence. We do, as well, though omit these results for brevity because they follow directly from what 
p
𝒛
 is measuring.

We limit ourselves to verbatim extraction in this paper; it is significantly computationally cheaper to do so because we can operate directly on model logits with one forward pass. (See Section 2 and Appendix A.3 for more details.) As a result, our work here (in several respects) only scratches the surface: there are various different ways to instantiate this extraction methodology in practice, we only explore a limited set of open-weight models, and we only test 
50
 of the nearly 
200
,
000
 books in Books3.

We further discuss how we determine extraction success for probabilistic extraction in Appendix C, where we describe how extraction success for different sequences can be aggregated into an overall extraction rate. In the main text, we discuss determining extraction success in Section 2.1.

Refer to caption
Refer to caption
Figure 11:Showing how the probability

 of generating a targeted outcome at least once changes as a function of 
n
 independent trials. Following the intuition of flipping a fair coin (where heads has 
p
𝒛
=
0.5
=
50
%
), we show how the probability of flipping heads at least once changes with more flips (left). We show how the probability 
p
 of generating the verbatim suffix of the “careless people” quote for Llama 1 30B (
p
𝒛
≈
35.2
%
) changes as a function of the number of independent prompts to the model with the prefix (right). These curves both use 
1
−
(
1
−
p

)
n
 (6), plugging in the respective 
p
𝒛
 (for the coin flip and for generating the “careless people” suffix given the prefix) and different values of 
n
 to compute 
p
.
A.3Computing sequence probabilities in one forward pass
Given that the core algorithm for 
(
n
,
p
)
-discoverable extraction is so elegantly concise, we include a (simplified) version of the code in this appendix. Our implementation differs from hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, which we confirm in discussion with the authors of that work. In that work, the authors generated the suffix a token at a time and summed up the per-generated-token conditional 
log
 probabilities. Here, we observe that this is not necessary. We can get the logits for each token in the sequence with just one forward pass through the model, and effectively only one line of code. (See Listing 1, line 28.) For a 
50
-token suffix, this results in up to a 
50
×
 decrease in compute—an enormous savings. (It is of course less with KV caching enabled during generation, but still nonzero). This also makes computing this metric cheaper than traditional greedy-decoded discoverable extraction [carlini2021extracting], which generates the greedy-decoded sequence (i.e., for a 
50
-token suffix, there are 
50
 forward passes through the model, even if those passes involve caching).

Note that this is the entire interaction with the model in our experiments using our probabilistic extraction procedure: for a given sequence, we perform inference on the GPU, get the logits tensor, and move the logits to the CPU for processing. (There are perhaps additional opportunities for improving the efficiency of our implementation that involve KV-caching and streaming logits, rather than performing a giant matmul. We will explore this in the future.) We are not making any changes to the model in our extraction measurements; we are just getting the raw logits from running a given training-data sequence through the model. (As shown in Listing 1, we do this for a batch of sequences at a time, yielding a batch of logits.)

The logits tensors we get are of shape [batch_size, sequence_length, vocabulary_size]. This is a three-dimensional tensor:

• Dimension 1: We have a slot for each sequence in the batch (with batch_size sequences overall).
• Dimension 2: We have a position for each index in the token sequence (with sequence_length
=
100
 being the number of total positions in most of our experiments).
• Dimension 3: We have a position for each token in the whole token vocabulary for this model (with vocabulary_size being the overall number of positions, e.g., this is 
32
,
000
 for the Llama 1 family).
So, at position [i, j, k] in this tensor, we have the logit (unnormalized 
log
 probability) value for the i-th sequence in the batch, at the j-th position in the token sequence, for the k-th token in the whole token vocabulary. That is, for a given token sequence in the batch (at [i,j]), we have the logit values for all of the next-possible tokens in the whole token vocabulary (a vocabulary_size-length list of numbers, one for each token in the whole vocabulary); these values can be normalized to compute the probability distribution over the next token in the sequence.

We then implement our sequence probability computations (i.e., compute 
p
𝒛
) as a post-processing operation on the logits we get from the model. (See Listing 2.) That is, for any given decoding scheme, we can transform the logits—for example, change the shape according to temperature, truncate them to the top-
k
—before normalizing them into the 
log
 probability distribution.

1import torch
2"""
3Runs inference to get logits for sequences z, which we will use
4to compute (n,p)-discoverable extraction (i.e., to compute p_z).
5 - dataloader: a torch.utils.DataLoader wrapping a torch.utils.data.Dataset containing the tokenized sequence z
6
7 - model: loaded transformers.AutoModelForCausalLM (in this snippet, assumed to have layers distributed across GPUs with device_map="auto", though for smaller models we use data parallelism)
8
9 - returns all_logits (logits indexed by batch number). In practice, we do not do this. We asynchronously do computations on logits on CPU to save log probs.
10"""
11def compute_logits_for_batch(dataloader, model):
12
13 all_logits = {}
14 with torch.inference_mode():
15 for batch_idx, batch in enumerate(dataloader):
16 input_ids = batch["input_ids"]
17 attention_mask = batch["attention_mask"]
18 seq_metadata_batch = batch["metadata"]
19
20 device = model.device
21
22 inputs = {
23 "input_ids": input_ids.to(device),
24 "attention_mask": attention_mask.to(device)
25 }
26
27 # Run inference; shape: [batch_size, seq_len, vocab_size]
28 logits_batch = model(**inputs).logits.detach().cpu()
29
30 input_ids = input_ids.detach().cpu()
31 attention_mask = attention_mask.detach().cpu()
32
33 all_logits[batch_idx] = logits_batch
34 return all_logits
Listing 1: Computing logits with one forward pass through the model. This is the key speedup in our implementation and this is the only code that interacts with a given model that we study in our main extraction experiments.
This computation is written out on the right side of Equation A.2. The key point here is that, for a given sequence that we are evaluating, all we do (starting at the first index in the suffix) is sum up the conditional 
log
 probabilities of the actual next tokens in the sequence, one at a time (Listing 2, lines 32-40). This is often called teacher forcing.

We work with the conditional 
log
 probabilities (adding them together), rather than the conditional probabilities (and multiplying them together), as this is more numerically stable to compute. However, note that these computations are equivalent. For example, consider the sequence "the orange cat" (where, for simplicity, we will assume each word is a single token). When processing "the", the logits reflect the probabilities of the next token. We find the logit value in the distribution that corresponds to "orange" and add the associated conditional 
log
 probability, and then continue on (doing the same for "orange", where we then get the conditional 
log
 probability for the next token being "cat"). We get the conditional 
log
 probability of the whole suffix by adding together the conditional, per-token 
log
 probabilities of the actual suffix. From the suffix 
log
 probability, we can easily obtain the probability 
p
𝒛
: the probability of the model (under the given decoding scheme we are using) generating that suffix conditioned on the prior context (the prefix).

Any given sequence should be low probability. That is, for a suffix length of 
50
 tokens, this computation is effectively multiplying together 
50
 per-token conditional probabilities (by adding together conditional 
log
 probabilities). For a sequence where each token has really high probability, this can still mean the overall sequence has low probability.

Consider that each token in a suffix has conditional probability 
0.9
. This is really high; it means that a single token in the logit vector (at each position in the suffix) has 
90
%
 of the probability mass; for a token vocabulary of size 
32
,
000
, that means the remaining 
31
,
999
 tokens all share the remaining 
10
%
. For this suffix, the probability of generating it is 
0.9
50
≈
0.005
—i.e., a 
0.5
%
 chance of generation. This is a relatively small number in the scheme of things; but it is also a really large number in general, if we consider what this means for the underlying token probabilities. In some cases in this paper, we observe sequences that have over 
90
%
 probability—an enormous number, especially considering what the underlying computation is.

Such unusually high probabilities associated with verbatim suffixes in the training data are indicative of memorization (where it is reasonable to consider 
0.5
%
, or even smaller, to be unusually high). As cooper2024files say, “the pattern [the model has learned from the training data] is the memorized training data.”

Also note that top-
k
 sampling truncates the logit vector to only the top-
k
 tokens (e.g., for a vocabulary of 
32
,
000
 tokens, there are 
32
,
000
 logits, but top-
k
 with 
k
=
40
 decoding will only consider the 
40
 highest-valued logits). This means that, if a given sequence has a token at a given position that is not in the top-
k
 of the logit vector, then we will not be able to complete the probability computation. This is what the has_impossible_token check is referring to in Listing 2, lines 36–38. In this case, the given sequence has 
0
 probability; it is not extractable with any probability.

In practice, our code deviates from Listing 2 for efficiency reasons. (The core logic is nevertheless the same as what we show here.) As we process conditional 
log
 probabilities, we save a lot of metadata in order to produce the plots that we include in this paper. For additional speedups (which, ultimately, total around 
200
×
 prior work on 
(
n
,
p
)
-discoverable extraction), as we compute logits on the GPU (Listing 1), we delegate our post-processing code to happen in parallel on CPU.

1import torch, torch.nn.functional as F, math
2"""
3For a batch of logits, computes the log probability (and probability)
4of the suffix given the prefix for a batch of sequences.
5 -inputs: a batch of sequences
6 -logits: a batch of logits (computed doing inference, above)
7 -suffix_start_idx: index in sequences where suffix begins
8 -transforms base distribution for temperature (temp) and top_k (k).
9 -returns list of logprobs (and probs)
10"""
11def compute_logprob_batch(inputs, logits, suffix_start_idx, temp, k):
12 input_ids = inputs_batch["input_ids"]
13 attention_mask = inputs_batch["attention_mask"]
14 if temp != 1.0 or k is not None:
15 logits = apply_temperature_and_topk(logits, temp, k)
16
17 # normalize the logits to a probability distribution;
18 # surfaces each next token’s log prob
19 # shape: [batch_size, seq_len, vocab_size]
20 log_probs = F.log_softmax(logits, dim=-1)
21 # log_probs[b, i, j]: for sequence at batch index b, log probs of
22 # predicting token j at position i in the sequence
23 log_probs_list = [], prob_list = [], batch_size = input_ids.shape[0]
24 for b in range(batch_size):
25 input_ids_b = input_ids[batch_b]
26 # shape: [seq_len, vocab_size]
27 log_probs_b = log_probs[b]
28 real_seq_len = attention_mask[b].sum().item()
29 total_log_prob = 0.0, has_impossible_token = False
30 # Compute log prob for each token in suffix conditioned on prefix
31 for i in range(suffix_start_idx - 1, real_seq_len - 1):
32 # get actual next token in suffix
33 actual_next_token_id = input_ids_b[i + 1]
34 # log prob for actual_next_token i+1 based on context up to i
35 log_prob = log_probs_b[i, actual_next_token_id].item()
36 # possible -inf from top-k filtering
37 if not math.isfinite(log_prob):
38 has_impossible_token = True
39 break
40
41 total_log_prob += log_prob
42
43 if has_impossible_token:
44 total_log_prob = float("-inf")
45 prob = 0.0
46 else:
47 prob = math.exp(total_log_prob)
48 log_probs_list.append(total_log_prob)
49 prob_list.append(prob)
50
51 return log_probs_list, prob_list
52
53def apply_temperature_and_topk(logits, temp, k):
54 if temperature != 1.0:
55 logits = logits / temperature
56 if top_k is not None:
57 # top-k values from logits; shape: [batch, seq_len, top_k]
58 top_k_vals = torch.topk(logits_batch, top_k, dim=-1)
59 # k-th element in top-k values for each item in batch
60 kth_vals = top_k_vals.values[:, :, -1].unsqueeze(-1)
61 mask = logits < kth_vals
62 logits = logits.masked_fill(mask, float("-inf"))
63 return logits
Listing 2: Computing logprobs
Appendix BTesting our measurement pipeline
To confirm the accuracy of our implementation of probabilistic discoverable extraction, as well as to benchmark its efficiency in comparison to the implementation in hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, we re-run the experiments from hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels for small Pythia models on the Enron dataset. To ensure consistency, we discussed the original run times and sample of Enron with the authors of hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels. We provide some brief details about these experiments to show due diligence for our implementation in our particular setting.

We ran these experiments with both float16 and float32, and report results for float32 to align with hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels. (However, we note no significant differences between the two.) In Table 1, we show the runtime for our experiments on the 
10
,
000
, 
100
-token sequences hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels drew from Enron. In Table 2, we report the same metrics as hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels: the greedy-decoded discoverable extraction rate and (as a point of comparison) the maximum probabilistic rate. We run these experiments on the same 
4
 A100s as all of our other probabilistic extraction experiments.

We successfully replicate the exact results in hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, which did not include the beginning of sequence (BOS) token in the sequences they use for their experiments using Pythia models. We compare to the extraction rates where the BOS token is included at the start of the sequence. Note that the probabilistic rate increases slightly with the presence of the BOS token.
We successfully replicate (exactly) the results of these experiments in hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels. However, in the process of doing so, we identified a small bug in their original implementation. The Pythia tokenizer is a GPT-2-style tokenizer, and does not add a beginning of sequence (BOS) token by default to the sequences it tokenizes. However, for our setting, it is best practice to include this token at the start of a sequence, as we submit these sequences as inputs for inference to LLMs (that are trained to expect this token at the beginning of the sequence). When we manually prepend this token and re-run the experiments from hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, we observe elevated extraction rates compared to hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels. See Table 2. We similarly prepend the BOS token in our Books3 experiments using Pythia models.
