C.1Setup
Data.
We perform our average extraction rate experiments with the Books3 dataset. We obtained this dataset from a previous (2022) download of The Pile [gao2020pile], which is stored on a university cluster and which we use for research purposes on language modeling.

The status of Books3 as a research artifact (as well as a training corpus for LLMs) remains unresolved. (See Section 3.) Nevertheless, this dataset remains widely available in the research community; for example, it can be found in the HuggingFace-hosted version of The Pile [hfpilededup], as well as in various other data repositories [e.g., books3a, books3b, books3c]. It also features as part of a benchmark task in the popular HELM evaluation suite.

For this set of experiments, we draw random samples from Books3, performing 
3
 separate runs each on 
40
,
000
 sequences. We discuss how we sample these sets of sequences in Appendix C.2.

Models.
From 
13
 different models, we try to extract 
40
,
000
 sequences from Books3 (
3
 times each). We test Llama 1 7B, Llama 1 13B, Llama 1 65B [touvron2023llamaopenefficientfoundation], Llama 2 7B, Llama 2 13B, Llama 2 70B [llama2], Llama 3.1 8B, Llama 3.1 70B [llama3], Pythia 6.9B, Pythia 12B [biderman2023pythia], DeepSeek v1 7B [deepseekv1], Gemma 2 9B [gemma2], and Phi 4 [phi4] (a 14B model). We group these models into three size groups: small, medium and large.

We use float16 for all models except for Llama 3 and Llama 3.1 models (and Qwen 2.5 models in sliding-window experiments). These models were explicitly trained to work with bfloat16.

We know for certain that Pythia and Llama models were trained on Books3, and can conclude from our validity experiments (Section 5 and Appendix E) that DeepSeek v1, Qwen 2.5 and Gemma 2 models were trained on (at least parts of) some books that are also contained in Books3. Phi 4 was deliberately not trained on copyrighted (whole) books, but we find that we can still extract some snippets from copyrighted books.

Metrics.
We compute extraction rates using Equation 7 for both traditional, greedy-decoded discoverable extraction (using the success condition in Equation 8) and probabilistic extraction with top-
k
 (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
) decoding (using the success condition in Equation 9) and 
τ
min
=
0.1
%
.

Compute resources.
We run all of our average extraction rate experiments in a slurm cluster environment, using the same node with 4 A100 GPUs.

C.2Experimental procedure
Sampling books from Books3.
To collect 
40
,
000
 sequences for which we compute an extraction curve for a given model, we use the following procedure. We sample 
4
,
000
 books without replacement from Books3. Then, from each of these books, we sample 
10
 non-overlapping 
100
-token sequences (
50
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes). We make sure that the start of the prefix text is a space (to prevent unusual tokenization). We run these 
40
,
000
 sequences through each of the 
13
 models to compute extraction metrics. We follow this procedure 
3
 separate times (i.e., for a total of 
120
,
000
 sequences across 
12
,
000
 different books) to get a sense of the variance across extraction metrics for different (empirical) population estimates. Each run of 
40
,
000
 sequences took between approximately 
8
 minutes and 
45
 minutes on 
4
 A100s. We omit detailed timing results for brevity.

Probabilistic extraction configuration.
We report greedy-decoded discoverable extraction and probabilistic discoverable extraction with top-
k
 (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
) and 
τ
min
=
0.1
%
. The maximum probabilistic rate (also reported in Appendix B) should be understood as a very loose upper bound on extraction of memorized training data; it counts all sequences with non-zero 
p
𝒛
 (i.e., it also includes sequences for which 
p
𝒛
<
τ
min
=
0.1
%
). For sequences with these low 
p
𝒛
<
τ
min
, it may also capture generalization.

C.3High-level takeaways
We defer discussion of validity to Appendix E, where we talk about how to draw conclusions for different models—some for which we know with certainty that Book3 is in their training data, and others for which we can conclude that at least some content that overlaps with Books3 was in their training data.

We attempt to use Phi 4 as a reference. Unlike the other models we test, Phi 4 was trained predominantly on synthetic data. Based on the Phi 4 technical report [phi4], Phi 4 was deliberately not trained on copyrighted (whole) books. As a result, Books3 (the specific corpus) was almost certaintly not included in its training data. Nevertheless, we are sometimes able to extract data that is contained in Books3. These sequences likely found their way into the training data from web-scraped sources that quote these books. We discuss this further in Section 5, Appendix E, and Appendix G.

Figure 12 shows average extraction rates grouped roughly by model size. As we expected (and discuss in Section 3), average extraction rates are relatively low. Using the very conservative threshold of 
p
𝒛
≥
τ
min
=
0.1
%
, the lowest rate we observe is for Phi 4 ( 
<
0.2
%
), and the highest is for Llama 3.1 70B (
∼
0.6
%
). Consistent with prior work, our results show that larger models tend to memorize more than smaller ones [hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, carlini2023quantifying, nasr2023scalable, nasr2025scalable]. This holds within the same model family. Phi 4 is an exception; despite being a 14B model, its extraction rates on Books3 sequences are comparable to the smaller (7–9B) models we evaluate. Small Llama 3 models memorize on-par with medium Llama 1 and 2 models. In general, later generations of Llama model families tend to memorize more Books3-contained text. For just one example, Llama 3.1 70B memorizes more on average than Llama 2 70B, which memorizes more on average than Llama 1 65B. This pattern does not always hold for specific books. We defer additional, related observations about Llama 3.1 70B to Appendix G.

Altogether, it is important to keep in mind that any given sequence that we deemed extracted is, by definition, a member of the training data; however, it is not necessarily a member of the training data because it came from the Books3 corpus. The sequence may be part of another source (or multiple other sources) in the training data. For Pythia and Llama models, the Books3 copy of a sequence may just be one of several duplicate copies in the whole training dataset. The same can be said for models like Gemma 2, where we are not sure if the Books3 corpus was included in the training dataset.) Nevertheless, it is clear from our results that sequences that are contained in Books—but perhaps originate from other sources—are included in the training data (Section 5 & Appendix E).

Appendix DSliding-window experiments with our probabilistic extraction procedure
We discuss details for experiments that use a sliding window across a book to surface memorization “hot-spots” at particular book locations. We discuss the overall setup (Appendix D.1), our sliding-window procedure (Appendix D.2), detailed results for 
50
 books and 
10
 models (Appendix D.3), our extraction coverage metric (Appendix D.4), and instances of extracted sequences (Appendix D.5). We describe other experiments in different appendices. We defer discussion of sliding-window validity experiments on non-training data and different prompt prefix lengths to Appendix E. Additional experiments concerning extraction rates and our reconstruction procedure are discussed in Appendix C and Appendix F, respectively.

D.1Setup
Data and book selection.
We draw our prompts (prefixes) from a selection of books in Books3; we verify extraction against the corresponding suffixes drawn from Books3. Books3 is a common pretraining dataset that is known to have been used to train several open-weight LLMs (Appendix C.1, [reisnerbooks3, lee2023talkin]. (See models, below). We list the 
50
 selected books in Table LABEL:app:tab:sliding-books.

We began our project with The Great Gatsby [gatsby]. This choice stems from a conversation in 2023 while writing a different paper [lee2023talkin], when Anthropic announced its 
100
,
000
-token context for Claude with the example of prompting with the entirety of The Great Gatsby [claudegatsby]. We then continued our selection process with the books listed with the associated plaintiffs in the (amended) class action complaint of Kadrey et al. v. Meta, Inc. [kadreyamendedconsolidated, pp. 4-5]. This is how we sourced the first 
13
 in-copyright books from Books3. We expanded to additional books by these plaintiffs, and also added in some generally popular books that we chose among the project team (e.g., hp1, hobbit, sisyphus, catch22) and some (less publicly popular) academic books based on our personal preferences (e.g., zittrain, dante). (This team, after all, is composed of academics.) We deliberately include selections from the public domain (alice, gatsby, ulysses), and an example of a book that was published under a permissive CC license (downandout).We added in alice because of concurrent work that was published on arXiv just as we posted our own [ma2025memorizationcloselookbooks]. To round out the list, we also selected books at random from the manifest file that accompanies the Books3 archive (e.g., onions, emirate, dungeons). Once we observed that certain very popular books were highly memorized by Llama 3.1 70B, we added additional bestsellers to our set (e.g., thrones, davinci, twilight, 1984, leanin).

Models.
Altogether, we run this set of experiments on 
17
 completion-style (i.e., non-chatbot) models. We includes results for 
10
 of these models: Pythia 12B [biderman2023pythia], Llama 1 13B, Llama 1 65B [touvron2023llamaopenefficientfoundation], Llama 2 70B [llama2], Llama 3 70B, Llama 3.1 70B [llama3], DeepSeek v1 67B [deepseekv1], Qwen 2.5 72B [qwen2.5], Gemma 2 27B [gemma2], and Phi 4 (a 14B model) [phi4]. For brevity, the 
4
 (smaller) models we do not include in our results are Pythia 6.7B, Llama 1 7B, Llama 2 7B, Llama 2 13B, Llama 3 8B, Llama 3.1 8B, and DeepSeek v1 7B. We note that Llama 3 8B and Llama 3.1 8B exhibit an enormous degree of memorization on some books, for models in this size class (e.g., 1984).

We load all models with float16—except for Llama 3, Llama 3.1, and Qwen 2.5 models. These models were explicitly trained to work with bfloat16. In an earlier version of this work, we reported results for Llama 3.1 that used float16. Slight differences in results in this version are attributed to the change to bfloat16. (We have otherwise confirmed our results are deterministic, with respect to the hardware that we use throughout.) Regardless, the overall results are similar, and the conclusions remain the same.

We picked these models because they each fit (with respect to 
16
-bit format) on 
4
 A100s, and because they fall into three different categories that we wanted to investigate: (1) models that we know with certainty were trained on Books3, (2) models where we have reason to believe were trained on copyrighted books (whether drawn directly from Books3 or from other sources), and (3) a model that we trust was not trained on whole copyrighted books.

For the first category, when we generate verbatim completions for these models on Books3 data, we can very reasonably conclude that we have extracted data that these models have memorized during training. We know that these data were members of the training data; extraction success (and thus memorization) is a standard conclusion in this setting [e.g., biderman2023pythia, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, prashanth2024recite]. For the second category, we would like to be able to do valid membership inference of copyrighted data. The third category is also to help confirm validity; as a baseline, we intentionally include a model for which we do not expect to be able to extract much (if any) copyrighted books data.

1. Category 1: Pythia 12B, Llama 1 13B, Llama 1 65B, Llama 2 70B, Llama 3 70B, and Llama 3.1 70B. We know that Pythia and Llama models were training on Books3. Pythia was trained on the Pile [gao2020pile] (which contains Books3), based on EleutherAI’s extensive documentation. We include only the largest (
12
-billion parameter) Pythia model.
Llama 1 and Llama 2 are known with absolute certainty to have been trained on Books3. The Llama 1 report explicitly says so. It came out in discovery during Kadrey et al. v. Meta, Inc. [kadrey] that Llama 2 models were all also definitively trained on Books3.

Both in documents in discovery and Meta’s otherwise public documentation, it is repeatedly noted that the development of Llama 3 continued to use the same data practices as prior versions. For minor-versioned Llama 3 models (like Llama 3.1), it is similarly documented by Meta in release reports that the same internal workflows were used, including for dataset curation [llama3]. While this is not as definitive evidence that these models were trained on Books3, based on what is public information about the training process and prior Llama models, it is widely believed in the ML community that Llama 3 (and associated minor versions) models were trained on Books3 (as well as additional books data from LibGen). We consider this sufficient to conclude with confidence that Llama 3 and Llama 3.1 were trained on Books3: Books3 text sequences were members of the training dataset. (And regardless of this reasoning, our validity experiments enable us to independently conclude this. See Section 5 and Appendix E.)

2. Category 2: DeepSeek v1 67B, Qwen 2.5 72B, and Gemma 2 27B. Although we do not know with certainty whether these models were trained on Books3, we aim to use our extraction methodology to assess whether they have memorized content from books. In particular, we want to design a measurement procedure that lets us conclude—with high confidence—that a specific sequence was in the training data; we want to be able to conclude that reproducing such a sequence in an LLM’s output is valid extraction (i.e., evidence of memorization). Based on our validity experiments (Section 5 and Appendix E), we can be very confident that when we successfully generate Books3 data, that those data were memorized from the training data. We discuss those experiments elsewhere, and note here that when we observe high probabilities 
p
𝒛
 for these models, we can safely conclude that those sequences 
𝒛
 have been memorized.
3. Category 3: Phi 4. For validity reasons, we also want to compare on a model that definitively should not have been trained on Books3. (See also Appendix E.) We pick Phi 4 for these reasons, as it is a very high quality for its size class and phi4 note in the Phi 4 report that it was trained predominantly on synthetic data:
Synthetic data constitutes the bulk of the training data for phi-4 and is generated using a diverse array of techniques, including multi-agent prompting, self-revision workflows, and instruction reversal. …[Phi 4 was also trained on] web content, licensed books, and code repositories to extract seeds for the synthetic data pipeline. phi4 (emphasis added)
Phi 4 is similar in size to Pythia 12B and Llama 1 13B, which facilitates cross-model comparisons of extraction risk. (This is because the amount of memorization, reasonably, also varies according to model size; see also carlini2023quantifying.)
Compute resources.
We run all of our sliding-window experiments in a slurm cluster environment, using the same node with 
4
 A100 GPUs. All told, this set of experiments took 
3
 GPU months. Altogether, this was the most costly set of experiment we ran. In total, all of our experiments took approximately 
6
 GPU months.

 books drawn from Books3 that we evaluated with our sliding-window probabilistic extraction procedure. See Sections 4 and 6, as well as Appendices D.1 & D.2.

D.2Sliding-window probabilistic extraction procedure
In this appendix, we provide more details on our main “panning for gold” extraction experiments (Section 4). These experiments take the approach of constructing 
100
-token sequences 
𝒛
 out of Books3 books, which we attempt to extract. These sequences are constructed using a sliding window across the length of an entire book, and we run these experiments for each of the 
50
 books in Table LABEL:app:tab:sliding-books. We use a very short, 
10
-character shift for our sliding window. This is deliberate; it is meant to help us, effectively, “pan for gold”—to identify regions within specific books where there are high-probability stretches of memorized content. The approach that we take reveals the position (in character space) in each book where these regions occur (and if they occur at all). We explain the intention behind these experiments, and how the associated results should be interpreted.

Sequence length.
We use 
100
-token sequences 
𝒛
, with a prefix (prompt) length of 
50
 tokens and a (target) suffix length of 
50
 tokens. We pick 
100
 tokens and a 
50
/
50
 split because this is accepted in the literature as a reasonable minimum for being confident that extracted suffixes are reflective of memorization [carlini2023quantifying, carlini2021extracting, lee2022dedup, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels]. 
50
 tokens for the prefix is less for validity reasons. Of course, this prefix length uses an amount of context that is equivalent to the output, and so is compelling in this respect. However, when we are confident that our measurements are capturing valid instances of extraction, using longer prefixes can help discover more underlying memorization. (Indeed, this is part of where discoverable extraction gets its name; carlini2023quantifying demonstrate the “discoverability phenomenon” with respect to showing how extraction success can increase with longer-context prompts.) We discuss this further (and include experiments with varied prefix lengths) in Section 5 and Appendix E. Other work uses shorter sequences (e.g., 
64
 tokens and a 
32
/
32
 token split [biderman2023pythia]).

Sliding-window sequence chunking.
For a given book, we start at the beginning of the text file in Books3. We sample a chunk of text that is sufficiently long to contain 
100
 tokens of corresponding tokenized text: to do this, we take a chunk of 
800
 characters, tokenize, take the first 
100
 tokens of the resulting tokenized sequence as the sequence, and discard the rest. (Each 
100
 token sequence is typically, but not always, reflective of 
300
-
400
 characters of text. Rare words and formatting make this vary considerably. This is why we use a character-chunk size of 
800
—to make sure we have sufficient head-room to always end up with a 
100
 token sequence.) We then shift 
10
 characters forward in the book text and repeat this process. We do this for the entire length of the book, which results in approximately one sequence every 
10
 characters, i.e., 
len(book_characters)
/
10
 total sequences 
𝒛
, for which we compute 
p
𝒛
. For example, The Great Gatsby has 
270
,
870
 characters, which results in roughly 
27
,
000
 sequences, for which we compute 
p
𝒛
 (Equation A.2). (See Section 2 and Appendix A.2.)

This means that the 
100
-token sequences overlap significantly. This is deliberate. We typically do not know how different open-weight models were trained; a priori, it is not clear exactly where we should begin sequences (or where they should end). With this approach, for extractable content, we expect to surface high-probability regions of memorized content, which we can then explore in more detail and more precisely in follow-up experiments. We picked a sliding window of 
10
 characters by running initial experiments with 
1
-, 
10
-, 
50
-, and 
100
-character windows. 
10
 characters exhibited an insignificant loss in extraction signal (at 
10
×
 cheaper cost than 
1
 character). We intend to investigate reducing this cost further in the future.

Initially, we did not expect this approach to result in high per-book extraction rates. This is because, even though this approach should surface memorization “hot-spots” by getting good coverage of 
100
-token sequences across the book, we are not being particularly clever about the start location for each sequence. Since we indiscriminately slide the window 
10
 characters each time, we expect that many prompts will begin in “odd” locations—locations in the middle of tokens, which lead to “broken” or otherwise “strange” tokenizations. We expected to yield near-zero probability of extraction.

However, with the final presentation of our results—in which we use a very high minimum probability 
p
𝒛
≥
τ
min
=
0.1
%
 in this work for deeming extraction successful (Sections 2.1 & 5; Appendices C & E)—this did not really have an impact on extraction rates.

So, in the subsections that follow, we show per-book extraction rates across the different models we test with this procedure. These are useful for very high-level comparisons of how many sequences we extracted with probabilistic extraction, compared to greedy-decoded discoverable extraction as a baseline (which, similar to hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, we observe consistently under-counts extraction).

Decoding configuration
Similar to hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, after some initial tests with different decoding schemes, we use temperature 
T
=
1
 for top-
k
 decoding with the 
k
=
40
. We believe that setting 
T
=
1
 makes the most sense for studying memorization, as this reflects the LLM’s base probability distribution. Since extraction is only successful when the underlying training sequence’s logits reflect very high probabilities (Section 2), we also think it makes sense to clip the full-vocabulary logit distribution to reflect some number of the top-highest probability tokens per step. This is the same intuition behind using greedy decoding for discoverable extraction—but with a twist. Greedy decoding deterministically picks the single-most-likely token per step. (This is equivalent to top-
k
 decoding with 
T
=
1
 and 
k
=
1
.) As the results in hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels show, this can be a suboptimal decoding strategy for measuring extraction, for several reasons:

• Greedy decoding is rarely used with LLMs in practice. Non-deterministic sampling schemes are more reflective of realistic conditions.
• Because greedy decoding is deterministic, the same prompt always results in the same output. As a result, greedy-decoded discoverable extraction provides (in this sense) 
1
-bit of information with respect to extraction: was it possible to extract the sequence? Using non-deterministic decoding schemes, we can get more detailed information about extraction risk; not all sequences are equally extractable, and this is useful information to analyze. (See Section 2.2 & Appendix A.)
• While greedy decoding picks the locally highest probability token per step, it is not guaranteed to result in the globally highest probability sequence (of the given length) under the model. Being able to explore more of the model’s distribution with non-deterministic decoding schemes can surface sequences (of the same given length) that are higher probability under the model. Such higher probability sequences can match the target suffix, where the greedy-decoded suffix does not (i.e., fails to extract that suffix).
• More generally, this allows for a bit more “wiggle room.” By being able to pick beyond just the top-
1
 token per step, the decoding process take locally suboptimal steps, which can still result in high-probability sequences that (even if slightly lower probability than the greedy sequence, with respect to the normalized top-
k
 distribution) reflect extraction. While we do not investigate this in detail in this work, we observe that (when this happens) such sequences are very similar to the greedy-decoded suffix; the greedy-decoded suffix is a near-miss for the verbatim suffix (it is near-verbatim). It may be reasonable under more relaxed (near-verbatim) metrics for extraction to have counted this greedy suffix as extracted, but that is not the approach we (or hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels) take, and so these suffixes would be missed. We intend to investigate this further in future work.
The above reasons also reflect the general motivations for using probabilistic extraction (rather than traditional discoverable extraction) as our metric (Sections 2 & 3; Appendix A). Similar motivations for useful extraction measurements are also noted in older extraction papers (e.g., carlini2021extracting, carlini2023quantifying); however, this work typically opts to use greedy decoding based on the perception that it is cheaper to do so at scale. This work does not claim that greedy decoding is the best or only way to measure extraction, and in fact notes [carlini2021extracting]—and in select cases shows [carlini2023quantifying]—that other (more expensive) decoding schemes are able to extract more training data. (Again, this seems likely because these schemes, like beam search [carlini2023quantifying], are able to produce higher-likelihood sequences under the model.)

Further, as we show through optimizing the work of hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, it turns out that it is actually much cheaper to compute probabilistic extraction (with any top-
k
 decoding configuration, including greedy/ 
k
=
1
), as we compute extraction probabilities as a post-processing operation directly off of the logits obtained from inference, rather than (as has been done in prior work [carlini2021extracting, carlini2023quantifying]) actually generating (greedy) completions. We can compute verbatim extraction probabilities with a single forward pass through the model, which is an enormous cost savings compared to generating 
50
 tokens per prompt. We discuss these details in Appendix A.3.

Note that because our implementation of probabilistic extraction makes computing 
log
 probabilities a post-processing operation on the output model logits on CPU (Appendix A), we also collect results for greedy-decoded discoverable extraction [lee2022dedup, carlini2021extracting, nasr2023scalable] by setting 
T
=
k
=
1
, incurring negligible cost to do so. For brevity, we do not include detailed results for this setting. To give a brief intuition for these results, we do include greedy extraction rates (with respect to the set of sliding-window sequences we construct) for each book’s results in Appendix D.3; these rates consistently under-count probabilistic extraction with top-
k
 decoding (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
).

D.3Book-specific results
For each of the 
50
 books we test, we include a separate subsection with the following information for 
10
 models (documented in Appendix D.1):

• Bar plot comparing extraction and generation rates. For each model, we plot three different rates: the greedy-decoded discoverable extraction rate; the probabilistic extraction rate for top-
k
 decoding (
T
=
1
, 
k
=
40
), counting a sequence as extracted if 
p
𝒛
≥
τ
min
=
0.1
%
; and the maximum possible rate of generating suffixes given their corresponding prefixes. Each rate is computed over all (overlapping) sequences from the sliding-window procedure (Appendix D.2). Because we use a consistent sequence set, this comparison is valid even with overlaps.
The discoverable extraction rate consistently underestimates the probabilistic extraction rate, even with this conservative 
τ
min
. The maximum rate of suffix generation is not an extraction rate: here we do not apply 
τ
min
 (effectively setting it to 
0
%
), so it includes all sequences with nonzero 
p
𝒛
. This captures not only valid extractions but also low-probability instances of spontaneous suffix generation given a prefix. We include this number for two reasons:

1. It is never 
100
%
, showing that not every suffix can be generated given its prefix, even in the limit of repeated prompts. This is important to emphasize for legal audiences, where there is a common misconception that, with enough queries, a probabilistic model can generate anything [toothpick]. Our results show that this is not the case.
2. We are conservative in globally setting 
τ
min
=
0.1
%
 to define extraction success. In future work, we plan to investigate more rigorous statistical methods for setting this threshold. As a result, our paper significantly under-counts extraction: the true amount lies above what we claim but below the maximum generation rate. Therefore, the maximum possible rate should be understood as a loose upper bound on extraction.
We return to these points when discussing the validity of our measurement procedure (Section 5, Appendix E).

• Summary table. This table reports exact values for the plotted rates, along with the runtime for the sliding-window probabilistic extraction procedure for each model.
• Histograms of extraction probabilities. We plot the distribution over suffix extraction probabilities across all overlapping sequences from the sliding-window procedure. These histograms illustrate how many sequences are extractable for a given model and how their extraction probabilities vary.
• Heatmaps of extraction probabilities by location. We also include heatmaps (as in Figure 3(b) and Section 6). At each character, the heatmap plots the maximum extraction probability among overlapping 
50
-token suffixes spanning that character. These visualizations highlight “hot-spots” of high-probability extraction and show how such regions emerge and taper off within a book.
If no sequences are extracted for a given model–book pair, we omit histograms (they would be empty). In one case—The Beautiful Struggle [struggle]—we extract nothing for any model, so both histograms and heatmaps are omitted. For histograms and heatmaps, results are presented in three groups corresponding to the categories of models described in Appendix D.1: models known with certainty to have been trained on Books3 (blue), models that were likely trained on books also present in Books3 (red), and one model (Phi 4) that, based on documentation, was not trained on whole copyrighted books (orange). See Section 5 and Appendix E for further discussion about how we can be confident that our results for all three categories reflect valid extraction.

Tracking down longer extracted sequences.
Visualizing memorization “hot-spots” also allows us to identify runs of memorized text from which we can attempt to extract more than 
50
 tokens. That is, in regions with very high extraction probabilities, these probabilities may be high enough to yield nontrivial extraction probabilities for longer runs.

We take the full list of 
p
𝒛
 for all sequences in a given book–model experiment, along with their start and end positions, sorted by start position. We filter to only those with especially high probability (e.g., 
p
𝒛
≥
50
%
), then merge overlapping sequences by taking the minimum start and maximum end. From this we construct longer candidate sequences to attempt extraction.

To illustrate why this works, consider a single 
50
-token suffix with 
p
𝒛
=
50
%
. Its per-token geometric mean probability is 
0.5
1
/
50
≈
98.62
%
. For this individual token probability, the longest sequence length 
x
 that maintains 
p
𝒛
≥
τ
min
=
0.1
%
 is

(
0.5
1
/
50
)
x
=
0.001
⟹
x
=
log
⁡
0.001
log
⁡
(
0.5
1
/
50
)
≈
498
​
 tokens
.
(However, for a suffix of this length, it would perhaps be reasonable to consider a smaller 
τ
min
 for determining extraction success.) If we see multiple sequences close by that exhibit 
p
𝒛
=
50
%
, we could perhaps find a much longer sequence that fits the conditions above (or something similar).

We applied this methodology to Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone [hp1] and 1984 [1984] with Llama 3.1 70B, given the extent of memorization we observe in those cases. Figure 13 shows one successful case: a 
300
-token extraction consisting of a 
25
-token prefix and a 
275
-token suffix with 
p
𝒛
=
36.1
%
.

D.4Estimating how much of a book is memorized by a model
We estimate book-specific memorization for a given model 
θ
 and decoding scheme 
ϕ
 with a metric for extraction coverage. We define this metric and provide measurements for the different models and books we evaluate.

D.4.1Quantifying extraction coverage
We compute extraction coverage as the fraction of characters in a book that lie within at least one suffix whose extraction probability 
p
𝒛
 exceeds a threshold 
τ
.

Definition 2 (Extraction coverage).
Let 
𝗌𝗉𝖺𝗇

)

𝒛

For details on 
τ

, see Equation 9 (Sections 2.1 & 5; Appendices C & E). In the main text, we omit 
θ
 and 
ϕ
 for brevity, since we always specify the exact model and decoding scheme being considered.

The results of our sliding-window procedure can be used to compute Equation 10. We pick a threshold 
τ
, then we filter the whole (sorted by start position) list of 
p
𝒛
 we obtained with the sliding-window procedure to only contain sequences whose extraction probability is 
≥
τ
. Our sliding-window procedure keeps track of character-level metadata so that we can visualize heatmaps; we have information about the start/end character indexes for the prefixes and suffixes. Based on these intervals, we can compute which characters in the whole book are covered with 
p
𝒛
≥
τ
, and then divide that by the total number of characters in the book.

High extraction coverage does not necessarily mean we can extract the whole book in one go, or that we have identified the minimal set of sequences to try to extract to get maximal coverage of the book. This is simply a procedure for estimating the proportion of total memorized text in a book, in order to do cross-book and cross-model comparisons, as in Section 4.2.

D.4.2Extended extraction coverage results
We display extraction coverage in two ways, which support different types of comparisons.

1. Model-level histograms. For each model, we plot the distribution of extraction coverage across the 
50
 evaluated books. This provides a high-level sense of how coverage varies across books, and enables comparisons of this distribution across models. For example, in the main paper we show two histograms for Llama 1 65B and Llama 3.1 70B at 
τ
=
10
%
. These results indicate that most books have little extraction coverage for both models at 
τ
=
10
%
, but with clear exceptions. They also reveal that some models exhibit substantially higher coverage than others—in this case, Llama 3.1 70B shows higher maximum coverage and more books with significant coverage, i.e., it has memorized much more book text than Llama 1 65B.
2. Book-level percentages. For each book, we compare extraction coverage across different models. This not only indicates how memorized a given book is by a particular model, but also highlights variation in memorization of a given book across models. We provide examples of this comparison in the table in Figure 4 (right), for Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone and Sandman Slim, evaluated with Llama 3.1 70B, Llama 1 65B, and Pythia 12B.
Here, we provide more extensive extraction coverage results. We include both types of comparisons for the 
10

=

). We set

=

min
=

%

As in the rest of this paper, we divide these results into our three model categories: (top) models that we know with certainty were trained on Books3, (middle) models that we can confidently conclude were trained on (at least parts of) some books that were also contained in Books3, and (bottom) a model whose developers claim was not trained on copyrighted books.
D.5Selection of extracted sequences
This project generated over a million extracted sequences. In an earlier draft of this paper, we included a brief selection of arbitrarily chosen extracted sequences from some of the 
50
 books in our sliding-window probabilistic extraction experiments. (See Section 6 and Appendix D). Given the current length of the paper, we omit these sequences for brevity. We provide some one-off examples of extraction in Section 5, Figure 13, and Appendix E. We also provide samples of the diff from our reconstruction experiments, which capture (near-)verbatim extraction in Section 7 and Appendix F.

Appendix EMeasurement validity
In this appendix, we provide more detail on the validity experiments introduced in Section 5. These experiments support that our measurements capture true instances of extraction. On this basis, we expand our analysis to models with undisclosed information about training on books—DeepSeek v1 67B, Qwen 2.5 72B, and Gemma 2 27B. Because our controls show that our method does not mistake chance generation for extraction, we can be confident that when these models register extraction success, it reflects memorization.

Given the length of this appendix, we provide a summary:

• Extraction of known training data is evidence of memorization. For models where the training data are known, high-probability generation of a verbatim suffix given its prefix is widely accepted as evidence of memorization [carlini2021extracting, carlini2023extracting, carlini2023quantifying, hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels]. Probabilistic extraction provides a more fine-grained version of the same test: rather than returning a single greedy continuation, it assigns a probability to the target suffix and reveals whether it surpasses a minimum acceptable threshold.
• We adopt a deliberately conservative minimum threshold. Probabilistic extraction produces a continuous measure of extraction, but requires a cutoff. We set 
τ
min
 very high to ensure we never over-count memorization. This makes our results lower bounds: we under-count memorization (Section 2). We capture more instances of memorization than greedy decoding, but we still do not over-count. We defer more fine-grained settings of 
τ
min
 to future work.
• Validating against non-training data The majority of our experiments—those on Llama and Pythia models—fit the case of extracting known training data from models. It is known that Books3 was included in the training data, and we are running experiments to extract Books3 sequences. But we can also do more to ensure validity: we can test our procedure on known non-training data, and show that suffix probabilities fall below our chosen 
τ
min
. We (and hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels) do this for an additional sanity check. In particular, we perform over 
200
 negative control experiments (Section 5.2 & Appendix E.4). Work on greedy-decoded discoverable extraction often does not do this; it assumes validity [carlini2023quantifying] or uses shorter prefix lengths to induce less bias on the output [nasr2023scalable] (Appendix E.6). Overall, our results confirm that our choice of 
τ
min
 is very conservative; we could set it lower without risking false positives. However, we are deliberately cautious, and defer this to future work (Section 5.3).
• Stress-testing extraction with longer prefixes. When validating 
τ
min
 on known training data, we can also ask whether longer prefixes make memorization more discoverable. Because suffix probabilities are conditional on the prefix, extending the prompt can bias the model’s output toward a certain part of its learned distribution (which can also further be affected by the decoding algorithm). As a result, as others have shown [carlini2023quantifying], for experiments on known training data, when a piece of training data is memorized, a longer prefix prompt can raise the probability of the target suffix and expose it as memorized (via greedy decoding). This memorization may not be discoverable with only a 
50
-token prompt, but may become discoverable with a longer prompt.
Our own experiments confirm these patterns, but also show clarify, for a specific work, that longer prompts do not always lead to successful extraction: if a sequence is not memorized, we are unable to find a long enough prefix to make it it extractable (Appendix E.3). This supports the conclusion that probabilistic extraction captures true memorization, and clarifies the limits of analogies that treat models as “monkeys at the typewriter” (Appendix E.5).

• Bringing the pieces together. Our validity experiments combine baselines, negative controls, and varied-prefix tests. For models known to be trained on Books3, we run baselines with different prefix lengths as points of comparison (Appendix E.3). We then add two sets of negative controls: (i) Phi 4, a model not trained on whole copyrighted books (Appendices D & E.4.1), and (ii) books published after the training cutoff for models trained on Books3 (Appendix E.4.2). Across more than 
200
 such experiments, suffix probabilities for non-training data are essentially zero, and the rare nonzero values are orders of magnitude below 
τ
min
. As a result, our procedure does not mistakenly register non-training data as extractable, reinforcing that what we count as extraction reflects true instances of memorization.
• Applying the procedure to models with unknown training data. Based on these results, we can also apply the procedure to models whose training data are undisclosed. For sequences that register as extractable in these models, we can confidently interpret them as evidence of memorization, regardless of whether the training-data source was Books3 directly or overlapping material from elsewhere (Appendix E.6). Importantly, as we state throughout, for our experiments on these models, our claims about membership in the training data are only about specific extracted sequences.
Appendix outline.
• Appendix E.1. Motivation for these experiments.
• Appendix E.2. Setup.
• Appendix E.3. Additional baseline experiments that for models known to be trained on Books3.
• Appendix E.4. Additional negative control experiments on non-training data.
• Appendix E.5. Additional discussion on “the monkey at the typewriter.”
• Appendix E.6. Discussion of how our work fits in with prior literature on membership inference.
E.1Motivating our validity experiments
The main point of these experiments is to validate that our measurements capture true instances of extraction, rather than chance generation of verbatim suffixes. They help us make confident claims about both our results for models trained on Books3 and our application of the procedure to models with undisclosed training data. Here, we expand on these motivations (Appendix E.1.1), connect our work to the literature on greedy-decoded discoverable extraction (Appendix E.1.2), and clarify the role of varying prefix length in our baselines and negative controls (Appendix E.1.3).

E.1.1Setting 
τ
min
 and extending to models with unknown training data
We can be fairly confident that DeepSeek v1 67B, Qwen 2.5 72B, and Gemma 2 27B were trained on at least some books: the Pile [gao2020pile] and several other common LLM pre-training datasets either directly include Books3 or other book corpora [lee2023explainers, lee2023talkin]. Indeed, other work on extraction with uncertain training data treats the Pile as part of a proxy for the true training set [nasr2023scalable, nasr2025scalable]. However, without disclosure, we cannot be absolutely certain. And without knowing whether Books3 was in fact included, we cannot—without additional validity experiments—make claims about memorization that are as strong as those we make for models known to be trained on Books3. This is because membership in the training data is a prerequisite for memorization; by definition, a model cannot memorize sequences it has never seen.

This is also why observing 
p
𝒛
 on non-training data is important for setting 
τ
min
. By comparing suffix probabilities from negative controls on non-training data (Section 5.2, Appendix E.4) with those from baselines on known training data (Section 5.1, Appendix E.3), we can draw a conservative line for what 
p
𝒛
 is high enough to be indicative of memorization.

That is, our negative controls clarify how to set the threshold that we use to count extraction success, 
τ
min
—the minimum 
p
𝒛
 at which we interpret our measurements as evidence of memorization. Running our procedure on non-training data, we confirm that the values of 
p
𝒛
 we observe are far below our chosen cutoff. That way, when we count extraction success for a given sequence with 
p
𝒛
≥
τ
min
, we can be confident that it does not reflect chance generation. Our results in Section 5 and the rest of this Appendix confirm this: 
τ
min
=
0.1
%
 is highly conservative——well above any 
p
𝒛
 observed in negative control experiments.

We therefore use these results to justify our choice of 
τ
min
 in this paper. More extensive experiments would be needed to lower 
τ
min
 or calibrate it model by model. Here, we prefer to be conservative: we would rather under-count memorization than risk over-counting, and defer fine-grained threshold settings to future work.

E.1.2Relating greedy-decoded and probabilistic discoverable extraction
To our knowledge, we run the largest set of validity experiments in the ML literature on memorization and extraction. Such experiments are not typically performed in work on greedy-decoded discoverable extraction [nasr2023scalable, nasr2025scalable, carlini2021extracting]. hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels conducted one small-scale test on non-training data for a small Pythia model, and used this to support validity claims for probabilistic extraction from much larger and higher quality Llama 1 models.

Given these norms, we initially omitted our negative controls from the main paper, deferring discussion of Phi 4 experiments to the Appendix and omitting our varied-prefix experiments entirely from the paper. However, after receiving some feedback about earlier drafts of this work that indicated serious misunderstandings about the nature of memorization and extraction, we realized that including the results of these experiments in the main text could help some readers better understand our results and claims. We now clearly delineate two sets of negative controls, both in this appendix and in the main paper text (Section 5.2): the experiments on Phi 4 (Appendix E.4.1) and the experiments on books published after training-date cutoff (Appendix E.4.2).

Here, we clarify the relationship between prior definitions and our approach, and explain how this motivates our validity experiments.

Greedy-decoded discoverable extraction.
It is widely accepted that successful greedy-decoded discoverable extraction on known training data is evidence of memorization; memorization remains the most parsimonious explanation [hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, carlini2021extracting, nasr2023scalable, carlini2025blog, cooper2024files]. In this procedure, greedy decoding always selects the top-ranked token at each step, making it a deterministic approximation of the most likely sequence. carlini2021extracting describe this choice as a tractable proxy for the 
arg
​
max
 in their definition of model knowledge extraction:

Definition 3 (Model Knowledge Extraction, from carlini2021extracting).
A string 
s
 is extractable from an LM 
f
θ
 if there exists a prefix 
c
 such that:

s
←
arg
​
max
s
′
:
|
s
′
|
=
N
⁡
f
θ
​
(
s
′
|
c
)
We abuse notation slightly here to denote by 
f
θ
​
(
s
′
|
c
)
 the likelihood of an entire sequence 
s
′
. Since computing the most likely sequence 
s
 is intractable for large 
N
, the 
arg
​
max
 in [the definition] can be replaced by an appropriate sampling strategy (e.g., greedy sampling) that reflects the way in which the model 
f
θ
 generates text in practical applications.

We add emphasis to two areas in this definition, which we discuss below. First, we note that this definition does not mention training data; it is just a definition about a model having knowledge of string 
s
. carlini2021extracting connect extraction of training data to memorization through a second definition:

Definition 4 (
k
-Eidetic Memorization, from carlini2021extracting).
A string 
s
 is 
k
-eidetic memorized (for 
k
≥
1
) by an LM 
f
θ
 if 
s
 is extractable from 
f
θ
 and 
s
 appears in at most 
k
 examples in the training data 
X
:
|
{
x
∈
X
:
s
⊆
x
}
|
≤
k
.

Successful extraction, under these definitions, is evidence for memorization. Ideally, 
s
 should be computed as the most likely sequence under 
f
θ
. However, since this is not tractable to compute in practice, greedy decoding is suggested as a reasonable proxy. It forces top-1 choices regardless of their actual probabilities under the model.

The relationship to probabilistic extraction.
Greedy decoding is equivalent to top-
k
 decoding with 
T
=
1
 and 
k
=
1
; one uses the base model distribution (
T
=
1
) and then only considers the token with top-
1
-ranked logit (
k
=
1
). Probabilistic extraction relaxes this by considering a wider set of high-probability continuations (e.g., top-
40
). This better reflects the model’s distribution and avoids the problem where greedy decoding yields globally low-probability sequences. hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels motivate their metric precisely from this point: the greedy-decoded suffix is locally optimal, but it may lead to a globally relatively low probability sequence under the model.

A hypothetical example illustrates this gap: a greedy-decoded suffix can have vanishing total probability, while a slightly different path within the top-
k
 yields a much higher-probability suffix. For this example, probabilities are computed with respect to the base 
T
=
1
 distribution. Consider a greedy-decoded suffix where the first token has 
99.9
%
 probability and is selected, and then every top-
1
 token after it for 
49
 tokens has probability 
1
%
; the total probability for the 
50
-token suffix would be 
.999
×
(
0.01
)
49
≈
9.99
×
10
−
99
. Now imagine we instead were not restricted to always picking the top-
1
 probability token; we relax this requirement to, for example, allow to select from the top-
k
 tokens. Again we consider probabilities computed with respect to the base 
T
=
1
 distribution, so that sequence probabilities are computed off the same logits. For the first token position, the top-
2
 token might have probability 
1
%
, and then the remaining top-
1
 
49
 tokens could each have probability 
99.9
%
; the total probability for this suffix would be 
.01
×
(
0.999
)
49
≈
0.001
 (or about 
0.1
%
).

The point is, by allowing for a bit more flexibility in the decoding strategy, the model can potentially produce much higher probability sequences; when we observe that such (high-enough-probability, 
≥
τ
min
) sequences match the verbatim suffix from the training data, then we count them as extracted. As long as we choose 
τ
min
 appropriately—i.e., such that it does not capture low-probability sequences that are perhaps reflective of generalization—we should obtain higher fidelity measurements of memorization. We should have fewer false negatives than greedy-decoded discoverable extraction. And notably, as we show in Appendix A.3, this is actually cheaper to compute than how work like carlini2021extracting computes extraction, as we can compute probabilistic extraction directly off of the logits, rather than needing to actually generate completions.

Greedy-decoding is a degenerate case of probabilistic extraction.
Greedy decoding can be seen as a degenerate case of probabilistic extraction, corresponding to 
τ
min
=
100
%
. Each step assigns all probability mass to a single token under a normalized top-
1
 distribution, even if that token’s true model probability is very low. Probabilistic extraction surfaces this mismatch more clearly and is a better proxy for identifying high-probability memorized sequences. Because it includes the greedy suffix when it matches the training suffix, it never misses those cases, while also capturing others that greedy decoding fails to expose.

E.1.3The role of prefix length in our experiments
We run our validity experiments with various prefix lengths, not just for 
100
-token sequences with 
50
-token prefixes and 
50
-token suffixes. This is a deliberate choice because additional context biases the model’s output distribution toward particular continuations. For sequences from the training data, this is known to surface memorization that may not appear for shorter prefixes. carlini2023quantifying call this the discoverability phenomenon: “some memorization only becomes apparent …when the model is prompted with a sufficiently long context.” Through the lens of probabilistic extraction, a longer training-data prefix can raise the probability of the target suffix in Equation 1, such that it surpasses 
τ
min
. (It is worth noting that any conditional extraction procedure—i.e., one that uses prompts, rather than unconditionally generating from the LLM—statistically biases the output. This is one of the reasons that extraction under-counts memorization; it only searches a part of the learned distribution.)

By testing various prefix lengths on known training data, we can estimate an upper bound on memorization (with respect to our extraction procedure) for a given work. We expect longer prefixes to expose more memorization, but that increasing prefix length will not typically induce all suffix probabilities to 
100
%
; that is, we will observe more extraction only if there is more memorization to uncover. It is not the case that one can simply make the prefix arbitrarily long and then extract any target suffix. We discuss this further in Appendices E.3 and E.5.

This logic also strengthens our negative controls. In this case, we should not be able to measure extraction for any unique sequences of non-training data—even when we intentionally induce more bias with longer prompts. Again, this shows that arbitrarily long suffixes do not all of a sudden mean we will register extraction with our measurements. Here, we should not be able to do so because the sequences were not included in the training data.

E.2Setup
Books3 books.
Running experiments with longer sequences is significantly more expensive. We therefore limit our results on varied prefixes to a small selection of books from Books3: Beloved [beloved], Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone [hp1], This Is How You Lose Her [loseher], and We Were Eight Years in Power [wewere]. We picked these 
4
 books because each represents (roughly speaking) a different pattern for memorization in our sliding-window results on 
50
-token prefixes (Appendix D) for the model that exhibits the most memorization: Llama 3.1 70B. This Is How You Lose Her is barely memorized at all (Appendix D.3.12. Beloved has fragmented memorization “hot-spots;” there are many throughout the book, but they are rather disconnected, separated by lots of regions that do not register any memorization (Appendix D.3.34). We Were Eight Years in Power has several “hot-spots;” some are fragmented, but some indicate long, continuous regions of memorized text (Appendix D.3.7. Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone is effectively memorized entirely, though not all suffixes have equally high extraction probability (Appendix D.3.39).

Non-training data.
The large majority of sequences in the in-copyright Books3 books above should not be training data for Phi 4; exceptions are highly duplicated text, like boilerplate copyright notices and occasional popular quotes that are included from other sources (e.g., the Bible, newspaper articles, earlier books). We also purchased a set of 
4
 books published in 2025—after the release date of the newest model we test, Qwen 2.5 72B (September 19, 2024). (See Table 5.) We run these books through models known to have been trained on Books3 as another point of comparison. Similar to the above note about Phi 4, none of the unique text in these books should ping as extractable. Exceptions are, once again, boilerplate text and popular or otherwise duplicated quotes from earlier sources.

Table 5:The 
4
 books that post-date training cutoff for all models we test. We use these books for one set of our negative control experiments. See Section 5.2 and Appendix E.4.2.
Overall, we want to see how changing prefix length impacts how much more memorization we can discover. As we increase prefix length, we expect to discover more memorization of Books3 text for models trained on Books3, and to effectively observe no major changes for Phi 4 for unique text from these books nor for unique text from 2025 books.

Models.
Following from above, we run these experiments on 
3
 models known to have been trained on Books3. We include Llama 3.1 70B, because this is the model that has memorized the most of all Llama models we test. We also include Llama 3.1 8B, as it is a much smaller model from the same family. It memorizes less than Llama 3.1 70B. (These results omitted for brevity in Appendix D.) While it may be possible that increasing prefix length for Llama 3.1 70B surfaces many more books that are effectively entirely memorized (i.e., like Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone), we do not expect this to be the case for Llama 3.1 8B, since it is a much smaller capacity model. Together, this will clarify how it is not the case that just making prefixes longer means that one could generate any target suffix they so desire—regardless of whether it was memorized.

We also include Llama 2 13B. This model is a similar size as Phi 4, which we use in our negative controls. They have similar capacity, but were clearly trained very differently. While we do not know all of the differences, we do know that Llama 2 13B was trained on Books3, while Phi 4 was not. For one experiment on non-training data, we also include Qwen 2.5 72B as a point of comparison.

Procedure.
We use the same sliding-window procedure as in Section 4.1, documented in detail in Appendix D.2. We inherit all of those setup details; the only difference is that we run for sequences of different lengths (and associated different prefix lengths) to extract 
50
-token suffixes: 
75
 tokens (
25
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes); 
100
 tokens (
50
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes, i.e., the typical setting); 
150
 tokens (
100
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes); 
250
 tokens (
200
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes); 
450
 tokens (
400
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes); and, 
850
 tokens (
800
-token prefixes 
+
 
50
-token suffixes). On our hardware, it takes approximately 
13
 hours to run We Were Eight Years in Power through Llama 3.1 70B with 
850
-token sequences (compared to 
∼
1.5
 hours with our typical setup with 
100
 tokens, see Appendix D.3.7). This cost is the main reason we do not expand these experiments to a larger set of books and models.

E.3Results of varying prefix length on known training data
We next provide extended results for baseline experiments that vary prefix lengths on known training data. Our results yield the outcomes we expect: we are able to surface more memorization with increased prefix length—to a point. Except in rare cases like Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone and Llama 3.1 70B, in which the entire book is memorized, arbitrarily increasing prefix length does not make very training data suffix extractable. This makes sense, as not all training data are memorized; our measurements should therefore not register all training sequences as extractable. These results also make clear that absence of extraction is not itself evidence that a sequence is not a member of the training data. Most sequences from Books3 are not extractable even from models like Llama 3.1 70B, which memorize a lot. So the claim we make with respect to absence of extraction signal is in this one direction. When we make claims about training data membership, these are either based on ground-truth knowledge (e.g., knowing the Books3 was in the training data and that 2025 books were not), or conclusions that we draw based on comparisons to our negative controls (Appendix E.6).

We include results for 
3
 different models on 
4
 books from Books3, as documented in Appendix E.2. The largest model we test is Llama 3.1 70B (Figure 115). For a point of comparison with a much smaller capacity model, we also include the smaller model from the same family—Llama 3.1 8B (Figure 116) and Llama 2 13B (Figure 117), since this model is of a similar size as Phi 4 (Section 5.2, Appendix E.4.1).

These results illustrate the high-level points described above. While for Llama 3.1 70B we are able to extract a lot more memorized sequences, we are able to extract relatively less from Llama 3.1 8B. That model has memorized less overall, which makes sense because it is a significantly smaller model. However, even from Llama 3.1 70B, for some books there is very little overall memorization that we can expose, regardless of prefix length. We can see this with the results in Figure 115 for This Is How You Lose Her. For Llama 3.1 70B, we observe diminishing returns for increasing prompt length. This suggests that there is a maximum number of sequences we can extract from each work, even in the limit of setting the prefix length especially long. We can show this with plots of extraction coverage (Section 4.2, Appendix D.4) as a function of prefix length, as in Figure 114.

E.4Results of negative control experiments
We provide additional results for our varied-prefix-length negative control experiments (Section 5.2) on both Phi 4 (Appendix E.4.1) and non-training data that post-dates training cutoffs (Appendix E.4.2). While these experiments provide strong evidence for the validity of our procedure, we would want to run much more extensive experiments to responsibly lower 
τ
min
 or to make it model-specific. We defer this to future work, and here opt to pick a very conservative global 
τ
min
=
0.1
%
, which we discuss also in Section 5. The experiments that we run here are of a more substantial scale than the original probabilistic discoverable extraction paper by hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels. That work performed one experiment on non-training data on a small Pythia model. The paper uses the results of this experiment as a basis to both validate the experiments on known training data in Pythia models, and to apply the methodology to much larger (and higher quality) Llama 1 and OPT models—i.e., to make claims about extraction of not-known-with-certainty training data, just as we do here with DeepSeek v1 67B, Qwen 2.5 72B, and Gemma 2 27B.

hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels finds that “…the number of queries 
n
 needed to generate unseen test data is orders of magnitude larger than for generating training data. [They] find that it is generally challenging to generate test data …This supports that, in [their] measurements of [probabilistic] discoverable extraction, matches between training-example targets and generated suffixes are almost surely due to memorization.” We observe the same pattern, though instead of discussing it with respect to the number of queries 
n
, as elsewhere, we frame our discussion in terms of 
p
𝒛
. In our results, the 
p
𝒛
 that we observe on non-training data are many orders of magnitude lower than what we deem extraction success in our experiments on known training data. That is, our measurements for extraction are not erroneously capturing chance generation of non-training data. As in hayes2025measuringmemorizationlanguagemodels, this gives us assurance that we can use our approach on models for which we do not know exactly what was included in the training data. We discuss this further in Appendices E.5 and E.6.

E.4.1Results on Phi 4
We run a large number of negative control experiments using Phi 4 [phi4]. As noted in Appendix D.1, this model was not trained on whole copyrighted books. It was trained on a mix of synthetic data, licensed websites, and public domain books. When running our sliding-window extraction procedure on this model for copyrighted books, we can expect (generally speaking, with important exceptions) that unique text from copyrighted books will not register as extractable with our measurements.

For our main results using the sliding-window procedure with 
100
-token sequences (Appendix D.3), we include Phi 4 as one of the 
10
 models that we evaluate. Here, in Figure 118, we show additional results for experiments on varied prefixes and 
4
 in-copyright books from Books3. We are able to extract non-trivial amounts of public domain books from this model (e.g., Appendix D.3.17). In contrast, we are not able to extract much unique text from in-copyright text. As elsewhere, we extract boilerplate text like copyright notices and publishers’ addresses. And we are also able to extract famous or otherwise popular quotes—either quoted in in-copyright books, or from the books themselves.

We (painstakingly) verify that every sequence that we extract from Phi 4 can be easily found in multiple sources on the Internet. That is, the quotes from books that we extract from Phi 4 are either in the public domain, quoted widely on blogs or platforms like Goodreads, or both. This is indicative (though not definitive) that these quotes could have found their way into Phi 4’s training data from these online sources, rather from the books themselves.

For further support, we can also compare the text at the book locations for which we extract these sequences to heatmaps other models. We note that, almost always, these sequences are extractable (sometimes with enormous probability) from all models we test. Again, while this is not definitive, it is suggestive that this text is highly duplicated in sources that are a part of common pre-training corpora—that they are not necessarily memorized because they are in Books3, but because of their duplicated inclusion in the training data from other sources.

We also observe similar challenges—to a somewhat lesser extent—for our negative control experiments on books published after LLM training-date cutoffs. These books sometimes cite earlier sources—other books, news articles, social media posts, etc.—that pre-date the training-date cutoffs and are widely duplicated online. So, even this seemingly “clean” case also requires significant manual verification to confirm validity, which we discuss in Appendix E.4.2.

Altogether, it is reasonable to conclude that these are true instances of extraction. These results reinforce that our procedure detects true memorization when it exists and does not spuriously flag text where memorization is impossible. Even so, these results show just how challenging it is to run completely clean negative controls for models trained on web-scraped data [lee2023explainers], for which duplicates of the same piece of text across various sources complicate what training-data membership means. A given book may not have been a member of the training data, but this does not mean that all text from that book was not. In these cases, it seems extraordinary likely that these sequences are members of the training data (and memorized). We discuss this further in Appendix E.6.

With the exception of the copyright notice, we are unable to extract any sequences from This Is How You Lose Her [loseher]. The sequences that we extract from Beloved are all posted (multiple times) on platforms like Goodreads. We discuss some of these quotes in Section 6.2 [belovedquote1, belovedquote2, belovedquote3]. Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone is excerpted and quoted in an extraordinary number of places on the Internet. We were able to find every extracted sequence on 
3
 different web pages (after which we stopped counting).

4

These experiments serve as a validity check that our measurements for probabilistic extraction are capturing true instances of extraction. By definition, no unique sequences in these books can be memorized because they are not members of the training data; we should not be able to (and do not) obtain high, non-zero probabilities—probabilities that we would consider to be successful instances of extraction for known or suspected training data. Note that, in contrast to our other heatmaps, we set a more permissive (i.e., lower) minimum for 
p
𝒛
 (
p
𝒛
≥
0.00000001
%
, or 
1
 in 
10
 billion generations), in order to see if there are sequences that can be generated even with fairly low probability that we would not count as successful extraction.
E.4.2Results on books from after training cutoffs
We run additional negative control experiments on 
4
 books that post-date the training of all models. As a result, these books could not have been included in the training data; we similarly should not expect to extract unique text from them for the different models we evaluate. However, as with our experiments on Phi 4, there are again cases where boilerplate text and popular quotes from other sources come up in our measurements.

Each computation introduces small rounding errors that accumulate and propagate to the final logits. When we exponentiate to to get probabilities, these errors result in only the exponent (not the mantissa) being reliable.

E.5Limits on “the monkey at the typewriter” metaphor
A common trope is to compare an LLM to a “monkey at the typewriter” [borelmonkey, simpsons]. The basic idea is that, given infinite time, an LLM could presumably output any continuation of a piece of text. Like the monkey, it could by happenstance output a pristine copy of a Shakespeare play. This metaphor seems founded in the idea that LLMs are probabilistic. The monkey is randomly typing letters, and so, too, is an LLM, since there is an element of randomness in the generation process. For a given piece of text (the context), the LLM samples the next token to output based on the next-token conditional probabilities. That sampling is random, with respect to the conditional probability distribution of the possible next tokens.

Already here, this description of what an LLM is doing to generate text should start to sound very different from a “monkey at the typewriter.” In that thought experiment, what the monkey is doing is random in the sense that every next character is equally likely to be produced at every step. But clearly, that is not what is happening with an LLM. The LLM has learned probabilistic relationships from the natural-language and other text (e.g., code) it was trained on. These relationships condition outputs given inputs. So while there is sampling involved—and thus probabilistic outcomes—those probabilities are not “random” in the same sense as the monkey typing. If an LLM could be used to produce a copy of a Shakespeare play (or Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone, Section 7 & Appendix F), it would not be because it behaves randomly like the monkey. (One could set the temperature to an enormous number, effectively inducing “monkey” behavior via the decoding scheme, but this is not how LLMs are used in practice.)

A “monkey” or a “toothpick”?
These probabilistic relationships can also be easily misunderstood in the opposite direction. One could imagine that a sufficient amount of context used as input would serve to bias the LLM’s output to a particular, deterministic outcome: with a long enough prompt of the user’s choosing, they could get an LLM to produce any particular, desired target output. This is the basic idea in Tyler Cowan’s “toothpick argument”:

If you stare at just the exact right part of the toothpick, and measure the length from the tip, expressed in terms of the appropriate unit and converted into binary, and then translated into English, you can find any message you want. You just have to pinpoint your gaze very very exactly (I call this “a prompt”).

In fact, on your toothpick you can find the lead article from today’s New York Times. With enough squinting, measuring, and translating.

By producing the toothpick, they put the message there and thus they gave you NYT access, even though you are not a paid subscriber. You simply need to know how to stare (and translate), or in other words how to prompt.

So let’s sue the toothpick company! [toothpick]

The “toothpick” misunderstanding of an LLM is mutually exclusive with the “monkey” misunderstanding. For the toothpick, prior context has a determinative impact on the output; for the monkey, there is no context long enough that could ever have a determinative impact on the output. Note that in the toothpick metaphor, it should also be possible to get an LLM to generate outputs that are exact copies of non-training data—in this case, “the lead article from today’s [paper]” (emphasis added). (One could, for an open-weight model, manually bias logits. This could force an LLM to produce certain outputs, effectively inducing “toothpick” behavior, but this is also not how LLMs are used in practice.)

Where our results come in.
There is plenty to say about misplaced metaphors about generative AI. We defer to others who have written extensively about this [lee2023talkin, cooper2023report, cooper2024files]. Here, we highlight how our specific results make specific points clear about why these two particular metaphors are both misguided. The key point is that our experiments show that (a) there will be at least some target suffixes that could never be generated by an LLM, even with infinite prompts to the model with a given prefix, and (b) a specific desired target suffix cannot always be produced by an LLM, given a sufficiently long prompt. The evidence for both comes from the same results: for a given LLM 
θ
 and decoding scheme 
ϕ
, some suffixes are impossible to generate, regardless of (a) how many times one independently prompts the model with a given prefix or (b) how long of a prefix one uses to condition the output.

Our results make this very clear in practice, but we can also make this point conceptually. Intuitively, for a sequence

We often observe
